Results are based on
100{,}000
iterations of the Metropolis-within-Gibbs outlined in Appendix
B.1
, discarding the first half as burn-in and saving every fifth draw thereafter. Point estimates are posterior medians and interval estimates are 95% equitailed probability intervals.

4.
Semiparametric Bernstein-von Mises Theorem

This section proves a general BvM, proving that
\Pi(\gamma_{n}\in\cdot|W^{(n)})
is asymptotically Gaussian and achieves the
Chamberlain (1987)
semiparametric efficiency bound.

4.1.
Assumptions about the Data-Generating Process (DGP)

I start with assumptions about the DGP. For notation, let
||\cdot||_{2}
be the Euclidean norm, let
||\cdot||_{op}
be the operator norm (i.e., the maximum singular value), let
\lambda_{min}(A)
and
\lambda_{max}(A)
be the minimum and maximum eigenvalues, respectively, of matrix
A
, let
\text{int}(B)
be the interior of a set
B
, and let
vec(A)
be the vectorized matrix
A
. A function class is Glivenko-Cantelli if it obeys a uniform strong law of large numbers (USLLN).

Assumption 4
.

1.
\{(W_{i}^{\prime},Z_{i}^{\prime})^{\prime}\}_{i\geq 1}
is an i.i.d sequence with common distribution
P_{0,WZ}=P_{0,W|Z}\otimes P_{0,Z}
, where
P_{0,W|Z}
is the conditional distribution of
W
given
Z
, and
P_{0,Z}
is the marginal distribution of
Z
; 2.
P_{0,W|Z}
has a density
p_{0,W|Z}
.

Assumption 5
.

1.
\Gamma
is a compact subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d_{\gamma}}
,
d_{\gamma}<\infty
; 2. there is a unique
\gamma_{0}\in\text{int}(\Gamma)
such that
m_{0}(z,\gamma_{0})=0
for every
z\in\mathcal{Z}
, where
m_{0}(z,\gamma)
denotes
m(z,\gamma)
at
P_{0,W|Z}
.

Assumption 6
.

1.
\sup_{(z,\gamma)\in\mathcal{Z}\times\Gamma}||m_{0}(z,\gamma)||_{2}<\infty
; 2.
m_{0}(z,\gamma)
is twice continuously differentiable in
\gamma
in a neighborhood
\Gamma_{0}
of
\gamma_{0}
for each
z\in\mathcal{Z}
,
\sup_{(z,\gamma)\in\mathcal{Z}\times\Gamma_{0}}||M_{0}(z,\gamma)||_{op}<\infty
, and
\sup_{(z,\gamma)\in\mathcal{Z}\times\Gamma_{0}}||\partial vec(M_{0}(z,\gamma))/\partial\gamma^{\prime}||_{op}<\infty
, where
M_{0}(z,\gamma)
is the Jacobian of
m_{0}(z,\gamma)
in
\gamma
.

Assumption 7
.

Let
\Sigma_{0}(z,\gamma)
denote
\Sigma(z,\gamma)
at
P_{0,W|Z}
. 1. there exists
0<\underline{\lambda}<\overline{\lambda}<\infty
such that
\underline{\lambda}\leq\inf_{(z,\gamma)\in\mathcal{Z}\times\Gamma}\lambda_{min}(\Sigma_{0}(z,\gamma))\leq\sup_{(z,\gamma)\in\mathcal{Z}\times\Gamma}\lambda_{max}(\Sigma_{0}(z,\gamma))\leq\overline{\lambda}
; 2.
\Sigma_{0}(z,\gamma)
is continuously differentiable in
\gamma
in a neighborhood
\Gamma_{0}
of
\gamma_{0}
for each
z\in\mathcal{Z}
with Jacobian satisfying
\sup_{(z,\gamma)\in\mathcal{Z}\times\Gamma_{0}}||\partial vec(\Sigma_{0}(z,\gamma))/\partial\gamma^{\prime}||_{op}<\infty
.

Assumption 8
.

1. the class
\{m_{0}(\cdot,\gamma)^{\prime}\Sigma_{0}^{-1}(\cdot,\tilde{\gamma})m_{0}(\cdot,\gamma):(\gamma,\tilde{\gamma})\in\Gamma\times\Gamma\}
is
P_{0,Z}
-Glivenko-Cantelli; 2. for every
\xi>0
,
\inf_{\gamma\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\gamma}}:||\gamma-\gamma_{0}||_{2}\geq\xi}E_{P_{0,Z}}[||m_{0}(Z,\gamma)||_{2}^{2}]>0
.

Assumption 9
.

Let
V_{0}(Z,\tilde{\gamma})=M_{0}(Z,\gamma_{0})^{\prime}\Sigma_{0}^{-1}(Z,\tilde{\gamma})M_{0}(Z,\gamma_{0})
. 1.
\{V_{0}(\cdot,\tilde{\gamma}):\tilde{\gamma}\in\Gamma\}
is
P_{0,Z}
-Glivenko-Cantelli; 2.
E_{P_{0,Z}}[M_{0}(Z,\gamma_{0})^{\prime}M_{0}(Z,\gamma_{0})]
is positive definite.

Assumptions
4
–
9
are similar restrictions to those encountered in the optimal instruments literature. Assumption
4
imposes the same sampling restriction as
Chamberlain (1987)
, and, relative to Section
2
, is a restriction on
\{Z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
.
Assumption
5
imposes correct specification, requires that
\gamma_{0}
be point identified, and that
\gamma_{0}
belongs to the interior of a compact
\Gamma\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d_{\gamma}}
. Misspecification is discussed in Section
6
. Assumption
6
imposes some smoothness restrictions on
\gamma\mapsto m_{0}(z,\gamma)
. It accommodates the differentiability conditions on
g(W,Z,\gamma)
that are routinely imposed in the optimal instruments literature (e.g.,
Chamberlain (1987)
,
Newey (1990)
;
Newey (1993)
,
Donald et al. (2003)
, and
Chib et al. (2022)
), while allowing settings where
g(W,Z,\gamma)
is nondifferentiable but
m_{0}(Z,\gamma)
is differentiable (e.g., quantile IV
(
Chernozhukov and Hansen, 2024
;
Chernozhukov and Hansen, 2006
)
). Assumption
7
imposes a compactness restriction on
\{\Sigma_{0}(z,\gamma):(z,\gamma)\in\mathcal{Z}\times\Gamma\}
and a mild smoothness requirement for
\gamma\mapsto\Sigma_{0}(z,\gamma)
. Assumption
8
.1 requires
Q_{n}(\gamma,\tilde{\gamma},p_{0,W|Z})
obeys a USLLN over
\Gamma\times\Gamma
, while Assumption
8
.2, combined with Assumption
7
.1, implies that the criterion
Q_{P_{0,Z}}(\gamma,\tilde{\gamma},p_{0,W|Z}):=E_{P_{0,Z}}[m_{0}(Z,\gamma)^{\prime}\Sigma_{0}^{-1}(Z,\tilde{\gamma})m_{0}(Z,\gamma)]
has a uniformly well-separated minimum at
\gamma_{0}
, an important condition for consistent estimation of
\gamma_{0}
. Assumption
9
.1 is a USLLN condition for the class
\{V_{0}(\cdot,\tilde{\gamma}):\tilde{\gamma}\in\Gamma\}
, and Assumption
9
.2 is a strong identification condition that, combined with Assumption
7
.1, implies
V_{0}:=E_{P_{0,Z}}[V_{0}(Z,\gamma_{0})]
is positive definite, so the
Chamberlain (1987)
efficiency bound,
V_{0}^{-1}
, is finite.

4.2.
Assumptions about the Prior

The next assumption concerns
\Pi(p_{W|Z}\in\cdot|W^{(n)})
. For notation, let
||f||_{n,2}^{2}=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}||f(z_{i})||^{2}
and
||f||_{n,\infty}=\max_{1\leq i\leq n}||f(z_{i})||
, where
||\cdot||
is
||\cdot||_{2}
if
f(z)
is a vector and is
||\cdot||_{op}
if
f(z)
is a matrix, let

\tilde{\chi}_{0}(W,Z)=-V_{0}^{-1}M_{0}(Z,\gamma_{0})^{\prime}\Sigma_{0}^{-1}(Z,\gamma_{0})g(W,Z,\gamma_{0})

be the
Chamberlain (1987)
efficient influence function (EIF) and note that
V_{0}^{-1}
also satisfies
V_{0}^{-1}=E_{P_{0,WZ}}[\tilde{\chi}_{0}(W,Z)\tilde{\chi}_{0}(W,Z)^{\prime}]
, let
\overset{P_{0,W|Z}^{(n)}}{\longrightarrow}
denote convergence in probability under
P_{0,W|Z}^{(n)}=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}P_{0,W|z_{i}}
, let
P_{0,Z}^{\infty}
be the probability law of the i.i.d sequence
\{Z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
(i.e.,
P_{0,Z}^{\infty}=\bigotimes_{i\geq 1}P_{0,Z}
), and sequences
\{x_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
and
\{y_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
satisfy
x_{n}=o(y_{n})
if
x_{n}/y_{n}\rightarrow 0
as
n\rightarrow\infty
and
x_{n}=O(y_{n})
if there is a constant
C>0
such that
|x_{n}/y_{n}|\leq C
for large
n
.

Assumption 10
.

For
P_{0,Z}^{\infty}
-almost every fixed realization
\{z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
of
\{Z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
, there exists a sequence of sets
\{\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}\}_{n\geq 1}
such that

1.

\Pi(p_{W|Z}\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}|W^{(n)})\longrightarrow 1
in
P_{0,W|Z}^{(n)}
-probability as
n\rightarrow\infty

2.

For each
p_{W|Z}\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}
,

(a)

m(z_{i},\gamma)
,
i=1,...,n
, is twice continuously differentiable in
\gamma
over
\Gamma_{0}

(b)

\Sigma(z_{i},\gamma)
,
i=1,...,n
, is once continuously differentiable in
\gamma
over
\Gamma_{0}
,

where
\Gamma_{0}
is the neighborhood of
\gamma_{0}
from Assumption
6
.

3.

The following holds:

(a)

\sup_{(\gamma,p_{W|Z})\in\Gamma\times\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}}||m(\cdot,\gamma)-m_{0}(\cdot,\gamma)||_{n,2}=o(n^{-\frac{1}{4}})

(b)

\sup_{(\gamma,p_{W|Z})\in\Gamma_{0}\times\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}}||M(\cdot,\gamma)-M_{0}(\cdot,\gamma)||_{n,2}=o(n^{-\frac{1}{4}})

(c)

\sup_{(\gamma,p_{W|Z})\in\Gamma\times\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}}||\Sigma(\cdot,\gamma)-\Sigma_{0}(\cdot,\gamma)||_{n,\infty}=o(n^{-\frac{1}{4}})

(d)

\sup_{(\gamma,p_{W|Z})\in\Gamma_{0}\times\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}}||\partial\text{vec}(M(\cdot,\gamma)^{\prime})/\partial\gamma^{\prime}||_{n,2}=O(1)

(e)

\sup_{(\gamma,p_{W|Z})\in\Gamma_{0}\times\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}}||\partial\text{vec}(\Sigma(\cdot,\gamma))/\partial\gamma^{\prime}||_{n,\infty}=O(1)

(f)

\sup_{p_{W|Z}\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}}\max_{1\leq i\leq n}E_{P_{W|Z}}[\exp(|t^{\prime}\tilde{\chi}_{0}(W,Z)|)|Z=z_{i}]=O(1)

for any
t
in a neighborhood of zero.
17
17

17

Use of
o(\cdot)
and
O(\cdot)
is appropriate because, conditional on
\{Z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
, these objects are nonrandom.

Assumption
10
states that, conditional on
\{Z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
, there are sets
\{\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}\}_{n\geq 1}
on which the posterior concentrates as
n\rightarrow\infty
, and these sets are structured enough to ensure certain smoothness (in
\gamma
) and convergence guarantees for
m
,
M
, and
\Sigma
. The assumption is general in that it is stated for an arbitrary prior
\Pi
for
p_{W|Z}
; I derive examples of
\{\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}\}_{n\geq 1}
for certain GPs in Section
5
. As for the contents, Parts 3(a) and 3(b) state that
m(\cdot,\gamma)
and
M(\cdot,\gamma)
converge to their true counterparts
m_{0}(\cdot,\gamma)
and
M_{0}(\cdot,\gamma)
, respectively, at a rate faster than
n^{-1/4}
under the empirical
L^{2}
norm. Part 3(c) states that
\Sigma(\cdot,\gamma)
converges to
\Sigma_{0}(\cdot,\gamma)
at a rate faster than
n^{-1/4}
under the empirical supremum norm, a condition analogous to Assumption 3.4(iii) in
Ai and Chen (2003)
. Requiring
o(n^{-1/4})
nuisance convergence rates is generally considered a mild condition in semiparametric applications. Part 3(d) and 3(e) are boundedness conditions that help show that
\tilde{\gamma}\mapsto q_{n}(\tilde{\gamma},p_{W|Z})
is a uniform contraction mapping in large samples, a property that helps establish that
\gamma_{n}
uniformly converges to
\gamma_{0}
along
\{\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}\}_{n\geq 1}
. Part 3(f) requires that the EIF has sufficiently thin tails over
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}
, a condition sufficient to control a remainder in an expansion of
\log L_{n}(p_{W|Z})
.

4.3.
Bernstein-von Mises Theorem

4.3.1.
Main Result

Assumptions
1
–
10
lead to a Bernstein-von Mises theorem (BvM). Informally, the BvM states that
\gamma_{n}|W^{(n)}\overset{a}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(\hat{\gamma}_{n},\frac{1}{n}V_{0}^{-1})
for large
n
, where
\hat{\gamma}_{n}=\gamma_{0}+n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{\chi}_{0}(W_{i},z_{i})
is a hypothetical efficient estimator of
\gamma_{0}
. Consequently, the posterior for
\gamma_{n}
behaves like a best regular estimator
\hat{\gamma}_{n}
for large
n
, thereby establishing its frequentist asymptotic optimality.

The key to the BvM is an asymptotically linear representation of
\gamma_{n}
. Stated as a theorem below, it shows that
\gamma_{n}
is asymptotically linear in
p_{W|Z}
with Riesz representer given by
\tilde{\chi}_{0}
. It can be thought of as a Bayesian analogue to the asymptotically linear representation of an efficient estimator, and validates that
\Pi(\gamma_{n}\in\cdot|W^{(n)})
accounts for the optimal instruments as
n\rightarrow\infty
.

Theorem 1
.

If Assumptions
3
,
4
,
5
,
6
,
7
,
8
,
9
, and
10
.1–
10
.5 hold, then, for
P_{0,Z}^{\infty}
-almost every fixed realization
\{z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
of
\{Z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
,

\displaystyle\sup_{p_{W|Z}\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}}\left|\left|\sqrt{n}(\gamma_{n}-\gamma_{0})-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}E_{P_{W|Z}}[\tilde{\chi}_{0}(W,Z)|Z=z_{i}]\right|\right|_{2}=o(1),

as
n\rightarrow\infty
, where
\{\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}\}_{n\geq 1}
are the sets defined in Assumption
10
.

Theorem
1
establishes that, asymptotically,
\gamma_{n}
is an (efficient) linear functional of
p_{W|Z}
. Consequently, a semiparametric BvM for
\gamma_{n}
can be established by building on
Castillo and Rousseau (2015)
, who prove BvMs for
unconditional
density functions with i.i.d data.

Theorem 2
.

Suppose that Assumptions
1
–
10
hold and that, for
P_{0,Z}^{\infty}
-almost every fixed realization
\{z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
of
\{Z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
, the prior invariance condition

(4)

\displaystyle\frac{\int_{\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}}L_{n}(p_{t,n,W|Z})d\Pi(p_{W|Z})}{\int_{\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}}L_{n}(p_{W|Z})d\Pi(p_{W|Z})}=1+o_{P_{0,W|Z}^{(n)}}(1)

holds for any
t
in a neighborhood of zero, where

\displaystyle p_{t,n,W|Z}(w|z)=\frac{p_{W|Z}(w|z)\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}t^{\prime}\tilde{\chi}_{0}(w,z)\right)}{\int_{\mathcal{W}}p_{W|Z}(\tilde{w}|z)\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}t^{\prime}\tilde{\chi}_{0}(\tilde{w},z)\right)d\tilde{w}}.

Then, for almost every fixed realization
\{z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
of
\{Z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
, the structural posterior satisfies

\displaystyle d_{BL}\left(\Pi\left(\sqrt{n}(\gamma_{n}-\hat{\gamma}_{n})\in\cdot\middle|W^{(n)}\right),\mathcal{N}(0,V_{0}^{-1})\right)\overset{P_{0,W|Z}^{(n)}}{\longrightarrow}0

as
n\rightarrow\infty
, where
d_{BL}
is the bounded Lipschitz metric.
18
18

18

For any two probability measures
P
and
Q
on a measurable space
(\mathcal{X},\mathscr{X})
, the bounded Lipschitz distance is given by
d_{BL}(P,Q)=\sup_{f\in Lip_{1}}|\int fdP-\int fdQ|
, where
Lip_{1}
is the set of functions
f:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow[-1,1]
with
\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}|f(x)|\leq 1
and
|f(x)-f(y)|\leq d(x,y)
for all
x,y\in\mathcal{X}
with
x\neq y
, where
d
is a metric over
\mathcal{X}
. Convergence in
d_{BL}
is equivalent to convergence in distribution.

4.3.2.
Comments about the BvM

Several aspects of Theorem
2
should be highlighted. First, Theorem
2
implies that some Bayesian point estimators and credible sets are asymptotically efficient. Let
f:\mathbb{R}^{d_{\gamma}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be continuously differentiable at
\gamma_{0}
and recall that
c_{n,f}(q)
,
q\in(0,1)
, is the
q
-quantile of
\Pi(f(\gamma_{n})\in\cdot|W^{(n)})
. Corollary
1
states that, conditional on
\{Z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
,
c_{n,f}(q)=f(\hat{\gamma}_{n})+\Phi^{-1}(q)n^{-1/2}\Omega_{0,f}^{1/2}+o_{P_{0,W|Z}^{(n)}}(n^{-1/2})
, where
\Phi(\cdot)
is the
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
cumulative distribution function and
\Omega_{0,f}=\frac{\partial f(\gamma_{0})}{\partial\gamma^{\prime}}V_{0}^{-1}\frac{\partial f(\gamma_{0})^{\prime}}{\partial\gamma}
is the asymptotic variance of
f(\hat{\gamma}_{n})
. Consequently, by setting
q=0.5
, the posterior median
c_{n,f}(0.5)
is first-order asymptotically equivalent to the efficient estimator
f(\hat{\gamma}_{n})
.
19
19

19

The restrictions on
f
imply the delta method preserves efficiency (see Section 25.7 of
van der Vaart (1998)
).
Moreover, the equitailed probability interval
CS_{n,f}(1-\alpha)=[c_{n,f}(\alpha/2),c_{n,f}(1-\alpha/2)]
, where
\alpha\in(0,1/2)
, is first-order asymptotically equivalent to an efficient Wald confidence interval
[f(\hat{\gamma}_{n})+\Phi^{-1}(\alpha/2)\sqrt{\Omega_{0,f}/n},f(\hat{\gamma}_{n})-\Phi^{-1}(\alpha/2)\sqrt{\Omega_{0,f}/n}]
, thereby leading to best asymptotic frequentist uncertainty quantification.

Corollary 1
.

If the assumptions of Theorem
2
hold and
f:\mathbb{R}^{d_{\gamma}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is continuously differentiable at
\gamma_{0}
, then, for any
q\in(0,1)
and
P_{0,Z}^{\infty}
-almost every
\{z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
,
c_{n,f}(q)
satisfies

\displaystyle c_{n,f}(q)=f(\hat{\gamma}_{n})+\Phi^{-1}(q)\sqrt{\frac{\Omega_{0,f}}{n}}+o_{P_{0,W|Z}^{(n)}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right),\quad\Omega_{0,f}=\frac{\partial f(\gamma_{0})}{\partial\gamma^{\prime}}V_{0}^{-1}\frac{\partial f(\gamma_{0})^{\prime}}{\partial\gamma}.

A second aspect is that Theorem
2
also holds unconditionally, where stochastic convergence is defined with respect to
P_{0,WZ}
. Corollary
2
formalizes this, and, by extension, implies an unconditional version of Corollary
1
(see Remark
6
).

Corollary 2
.

If the assumptions of Theorem
2
holds, then

\displaystyle d_{BL}\left(\Pi\left(\sqrt{n}(\gamma_{n}-\hat{\gamma}_{n})\in\cdot\middle|W^{(n)}\right),\mathcal{N}\left(0,V_{0}^{-1}\right)\right)\overset{P_{0,WZ}^{n}}{\longrightarrow}0

as
n\rightarrow\infty
, where
P_{0,WZ}^{n}
is
n
-fold product of
P_{0,WZ}
.

A final aspect worth discussing is condition (
4
). These conditions are common in semiparametric BvMs (see
Castillo (2012)
;
Castillo (2024)
,
Rivoirard and Rousseau (2012a)
,
Castillo and Rousseau (2015)
,
Ray and van der Vaart (2020)
, and
Monard et al. (2021)
, to name a few), and arise from expanding the log-likelihood (
\log L_{n}(p_{W|Z})
) around the least favorable parametric submodel (
p_{t,n,W|Z}
). The name ‘prior invariance’ reflects that its verification typically requires the prior be roughly unchanged under shifts in the direction of the EIF. Indeed, Section
5
verifies (
4
) for GPs by finding a sequence
\{\tilde{g}_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
that uniformly approximates the
Chamberlain (1987)
optimal residual
\tilde{g}_{0}=-V_{0}\tilde{\chi}_{0}
, with
o(n^{-1/4})
approximation error, and for which the law
\Pi_{n,t}
of
p_{n,t,W|Z}\propto p_{W|Z}\exp(-t^{\prime}V_{0}^{-1}\tilde{g}_{n}/\sqrt{n})
under
\Pi
satisfies
\Pi_{n,t}\ll\Pi
and
d\Pi_{n,t}/d\Pi\rightarrow 1
uniformly along
\{\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}\}_{n\geq 1}
as
n\rightarrow\infty
. For the Matérn process (i.e., Example
2
), this restricts the GP sample path smoothness relative to
\tilde{g}_{0}
.

5.
Verifying Assumption
10
and Prior Invariance with GPs

I provide sufficient conditions for Assumption
10
and (
4
) for the priors from Section
2.2
.

5.1.
Setup and Assumptions

Recall
p_{\theta,B}(w|z)\propto f_{\theta}(w|z)\exp(B(F_{\theta,z}(w),F_{0}(z)))
, where
f_{\theta}(\cdot|z)
,
\theta\in\Theta\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d_{\theta}}
with
d_{\theta}<\infty
, is a conditional density,
F_{\theta,z}(\cdot)
is the
d_{w}\times 1
vector of conditional CDFs attached to
f_{\theta}(\cdot|z)
,
F_{0}:\mathcal{Z}\rightarrow[0,1]^{d_{z}}
is a known invertible transformation, and
B:T\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a function. A prior for
p_{\theta,B}
is obtained from a prior
\Pi
for
(\theta,B)
. Also, recall that
(C^{\alpha}([0,1]^{d},\mathbb{R}),||\cdot||_{\alpha})
and
(S^{\alpha}([0,1]^{d},\mathbb{R}),||\cdot||_{2,2,\alpha})
denote Hölder and Sobolev spaces, respectively, of order
\alpha>0
(see Footnote
12
for precise details).

Assumption 11
.

1.
\mathcal{W}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d_{w}}
is compact; 2.
\mathcal{Z}=[0,1]^{d_{z}}
; 3.
p_{0,W|Z}
is continuous and positive on
\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{Z}
; 4.
P_{0,Z}
admits a Lebesgue density
p_{0,Z}
that is bounded away from zero and infinity.

Assumption 12
.

1.
(w,z,\gamma)\mapsto g_{j}(w,z,\gamma)
is continuous in all arguments over
\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{Z}\times\Gamma
for
j=1,...,d_{g}
; 2.
g_{j}(w,z,\gamma)
,
j=1,...,d_{g}
, is differentiable in
\gamma
over a neighborhood
\Gamma_{0}
of
\gamma_{0}
with first and second derivatives continuous in all arguments over
\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{Z}\times\Gamma_{0}
.

Assumption 13
.

1.
\Theta\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d_{\theta}}
is compact; 2.
(w,z,\theta)\mapsto f_{\theta,j}(w_{j}|w_{1},...,w_{j-1},z)
is continuous in all arguments for all
j=1,...,d_{w}
; 3.
f_{\theta,j}(w_{j}|w_{1},...,w_{j-1},z)>0
for all
(w,z,\theta)\in\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{Z}\times\Theta
and all
j=1,...,d_{w}
; 4. there is a constant
L>0
such that
\sup_{(w,z)\in\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{Z}}|\log f_{\theta_{1}}(w|z)-\log f_{\theta_{2}}(w|z)|\leq L||\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}||_{2}
for all
\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\in\Theta
.
20
20

20

Recall that
f_{\theta,j}(w_{j}|w_{1},...,w_{j-1},z)
is the density of
W_{j}
given
\{W_{j^{\prime}}\}_{j^{\prime}=1}^{j-1}
and
Z
under
f_{\theta}(w|z)
.

Assumption 14
.

\Pi=\Pi_{\Theta}\otimes\Pi_{\mathcal{B}}
, where 1.
\Pi_{\Theta}
is a probability distribution over
\Theta
with a continuous Lebesgue density
\pi_{\Theta}
, and 2.
\Pi_{\mathcal{B}}
is the law
\tilde{\Pi}_{\mathcal{B}}
of a centered Gaussian process
B
in
(C([0,1]^{d},\mathbb{R}),||\cdot||_{\infty})
conditioned on
\{||B||_{\infty}\leq\bar{B}\}
for some large
\bar{B}\in(0,\infty)
with
B\sim\tilde{\Pi}_{\mathcal{B}}
taking values in
(C^{a}([0,1]^{d},\mathbb{R}),||\cdot||_{a})
for every
0<a<\alpha
and
\alpha>d_{z}/2
.

Assumptions
11
–
14
comprise
sufficient
conditions on the DGP and prior that will be used to verify Assumption
10
. The main limitation of Assumption
11
is compactness of
\mathcal{W}
, however it is not overly restrictive relative to the literature on semiparametric BvMs for density functionals
(
Rivoirard and Rousseau, 2012a
;
Castillo and Rousseau, 2015
)
, and, more broadly, posterior consistency with infinite-dimensional exponential families
(
Scricciolo, 2006
;
Rivoirard and Rousseau, 2012b
)
. I expect compact
\mathcal{W}
can be relaxed (see Footnote
23
). Compactness of
\mathcal{Z}
is a standard assumption in nonparametric conditional density estimation, and, given such an assumption, setting
\mathcal{Z}=[0,1]^{d_{z}}
is without loss of generality (and enables setting
F_{0}(z)=z
). Assumption
12
is also stronger than the high-level smoothness conditions imposed in Section
4
, as it rules out models with nondifferentiable
g(W,Z,\gamma)
. Assumption
13
imposes regularity conditions on
\{f_{\theta}:\theta\in\Theta\}
that are satisfied, for example, when
f_{\theta}
is a Gaussian linear regression (truncated to
\mathcal{W}
),
\mathcal{Z}
is compact (i.e. Assumption
11
.2), and
\Theta
constrains the slope and variance to compact sets, with the variance bounded away from zero. Assumption
14
is a sample path smoothness restriction that many Gaussian processes satisfy (e.g., the Matérn process from Example
2
). Uniformly bounding
B
is a technical device used to control random approximation errors, and, since the magnitude of
\bar{B}
has no role, it can be viewed as an arbitrarily large but finite number.
21
21

21

Lemma 5.1 in
van der Vaart and van Zanten (2008b)
shows
\tilde{\Pi}_{\mathcal{B}}(||B||_{\infty}\leq\bar{B})>0
for any
\bar{B}>0
.

5.2.
Verifying Assumption
10

Under Assumptions
11
and
12
, only Assumptions
10
.3(a)–10.3(c) need verification. Let
h(p_{\theta,B}(\cdot|z),p_{0,W|Z}(\cdot|z))
be the Hellinger distance between
p_{\theta,B}
and
p_{0,W|Z}
at
z
, and let
h_{n,2}(p_{\theta,B},p_{0,W|Z})=(n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}h^{2}(p_{\theta,B}(\cdot|z_{i}),p_{0,W|Z}(\cdot|z_{i})))^{1/2}
be the root mean square Hellinger (RMSH) distance.
22
22

22

h(p_{\theta,B}(\cdot|z),p_{0,W|Z}(\cdot|z))
satisfies
h^{2}(p_{\theta,B}(\cdot|z),p_{0,W|Z}(\cdot|z))=\int_{\mathcal{W}}(p_{\theta,B}^{1/2}(w|z)-p_{0,W|Z}^{1/2}(w|z))^{2}dw
.
Under Assumptions
5
.1,
11
,
12
, the uniform Lipschitz conditions hold:
\sup_{\gamma\in\Gamma}||m(\cdot,\gamma)-m_{0}(\cdot,\gamma)||_{n,2}\leq C_{1}h_{n,2}(p_{\theta,B},p_{0,W|Z})
and
\sup_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{0}}||M(\cdot,\gamma)-M_{0}(\cdot,\gamma)||_{n,2}\leq C_{2}h_{n,2}(p_{\theta,B},p_{0,W|Z})
for constants
C_{1},C_{2}>0
. Consequently, a RMSH convergence rate is an upper bound on the rate of convergence for
m(\cdot,\gamma)
and
M(\cdot,\gamma)
to
m_{0}(\cdot,\gamma)
and
M_{0}(\cdot,\gamma)
, respectively, in the empirical
L^{2}
norm.
23
23

23

This Lipschitz property (and the resulting convergence implications) seems to be where compactness of
\mathcal{W}
is most crucial. Subject to modified restrictions on
\{f_{\theta}:\theta\in\Theta\}
, I conjecture that compactness could be relaxed the following ways: (i) boundedness of
g_{j}(W,Z,\gamma)
and its derivatives in
\gamma
, (ii) uniform integrability of
\{g_{j}(W,z,\gamma):(z,\gamma)\in\mathcal{Z}\times\Gamma\}
(and similarly the derivatives in
\gamma
, except replacing
\Gamma
with
\Gamma_{0}
) with respect to
p_{0,W|Z}
and
\{p_{\theta,B}:\theta\in\Theta,\ ||B||_{\infty}\leq\bar{B}\}
(or a subset on which the posterior concentrates as
n\rightarrow\infty
), or (iii) convergence around
p_{0,W|Z}
under a stronger norm (e.g.,
L^{2}
). The intuition is that (i) and (ii) preserves RMSH convergence implying
||\cdot||_{n,2}
-convergence of conditional expectations, while (iii) could relax boundedness via, in the case of
L^{2}
, the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality.
This means that Assumptions
10
.3(a) and
10
.3(b) are verified if the RMSH rate is
o(n^{-1/4})
(by restricting
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}
to be contained in an appropriately shrinking RMSH ball around
p_{0,W|Z}
).

Let
\mathcal{H}
be the Reproducing Kernel Hilbert Space (RKHS) of the unrestricted centered GP (i.e., corresponding to
\tilde{\Pi}_{\mathcal{B}}
), and let
||\cdot||_{\mathcal{H}}
denote the RKHS norm (i.e.,
||\cdot||_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}=\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
, where
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
is the RKHS inner product).
24
24

24

See
van der Vaart and van Zanten (2008b)
for a formal definition of the RKHS of a centered GP.
The RMSH rate is determined by the RKHS. To formalize this, recall that
b_{0,\theta}(u,z)=\log p_{0,W|Z}(F_{\theta,z}^{-1}(u)|z)-\log f_{\theta}(F_{\theta,z}^{-1}(u)|z)
for each
(u^{\prime},z^{\prime})^{\prime}\in[0,1]^{d}
, and define the
supremum norm concentration function
at
b_{0,\theta}
as

\displaystyle\varphi_{b_{0,\theta}}(\delta)=\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}:||h-b_{0,\theta}||_{\infty}<\delta}\frac{1}{2}||h||_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}-\log\tilde{\Pi}_{\mathcal{B}}(||B||_{\infty}<\delta)

for
\delta>0
. The concentration function uses the RKHS to measure the prior mass around
b_{0,\theta}
(see Lemma 5.3 in
van der Vaart and van Zanten (2008b)
). Building on
Ghosal and van der Vaart (2007)
and
van der Vaart and van Zanten (2008a)
, Proposition
1
states that the RMSH rate
\delta_{n}
satisfies
\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\varphi_{b_{0,\theta}}(\delta_{n})\leq n\delta_{n}^{2}
. For notation, let
\overline{\mathcal{H}}
denote the
||\cdot||_{\infty}
-closure of
\mathcal{H}
.

Proposition 1
.

Suppose Assumptions
4
,
11
,
13
, and
14
holds. If 1.
\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}||b_{0,\theta}||_{\infty}\leq\bar{B}
, 2.
\{b_{0,\theta}:\theta\in\Theta\}\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{H}}
, and 3.
\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\varphi_{b_{0,\theta}}(\delta_{n})\leq n\delta_{n}^{2}
for some
\{\delta_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
satisfying
\delta_{n}\rightarrow 0
and
n\delta_{n}^{2}\rightarrow\infty
as
n\rightarrow\infty
, then, for
P_{0,Z}^{\infty}
-almost every fixed realization
\{z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
of
\{Z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
,
\Pi(h_{n,2}(p_{\theta,B},p_{0,W|Z})<D\delta_{n}|W^{(n)})\rightarrow 1
in
P_{0,W|Z}^{(n)}
-probability as
n\rightarrow\infty
for some
D>0
.

Proposition
1
is not sufficient for Assumption
10
.3(c). Proposition
2
below proves that, under smoothness conditions, an RMSH rate leads to a convergence rate in the empirical supremum Hellinger distance
h_{n,\infty}(p_{\theta,B},p_{0,W|Z})=\max_{1\leq i\leq n}h(p_{\theta,B}(\cdot|z_{i}),p_{0,W|Z}(\cdot|z_{i}))
.
25
25

25

The smoothness conditions on
f_{\theta}
hold, for example, if
f_{\theta}(w|z)
is a homoskedastic Gaussian linear regression (truncated to
\mathcal{W}
), with bounded
z
, and with compactly supported slopes and variances (and variances bounded away from zero).
By Assumptions
5
,
11
, and
12
, this leads to conditions for Assumption
10
.3(c) by a similar Lipschitz property (i.e.,
\sup_{\gamma\in\Gamma}||\Sigma(\cdot,\gamma)-\Sigma_{0}(\cdot,\gamma)||_{n,\infty}\leq C_{3}h_{n,\infty}(p_{\theta,B},p_{0,W|Z})
for some
C_{3}>0
).
26
26

26

A similar conjecture to Footnote
23
applies.
It is important to note the
h_{n,\infty}
rate is
only
an upper bound and sharper convergence rates may be possible; I leave full treatment of optimal
h_{n,\infty}
rates for future research because, to the best of my knowledge, posterior contraction rates in the supremum distance for conditional densities is an open question (and is of general statistical interest).

Proposition 2
.

Suppose Assumptions
4
,
11
,
13
, and
14
hold, the conditions of Proposition
1
hold,
p_{0,W|Z}
satisfies
\int_{\mathcal{W}}||\log p_{0,W|Z}(w|\cdot)||_{\alpha_{0}}dw<\infty
for some
\alpha_{0}>d_{z}/2
, and
\{f_{\theta}:\theta\in\Theta\}
satisfies
\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\int_{\mathcal{W}}||\log f_{\theta}(w|\cdot)||_{a}dw<\infty
and
\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\int_{\mathcal{W}}||F_{\theta,\cdot}(w)||_{a}^{\lfloor a\rfloor}dw<\infty
for each
a\in(0,\alpha)
. Then, for
P_{0,Z}^{\infty}
-almost every fixed realization
\{z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
of
\{Z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
, there exists
\{\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{1,n,W|Z}\}_{n\geq 1}
and
\{\tilde{\delta}_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
such that
\Pi(\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,1,W|Z}|W^{(n)})\rightarrow 1
in
P_{0,W|Z}^{(n)}
-probability as
n\rightarrow\infty
and
\sup_{p_{\theta,B}\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{1,n,W|Z}}h_{n,\infty}(p_{\theta,B},p_{0,W|Z})\leq\tilde{D}\tilde{\delta}_{n}
for a constant
\tilde{D}>0
, where
\tilde{\delta}_{n}
depends on
\alpha,\alpha_{0}
,
d_{z}
, and the RMSH rate
\delta_{n}
.

Example 2

(Continued)
.

Suppose
\{b_{0,\theta}:\theta\in\Theta\}\subseteq C^{\alpha_{0}}([0,1]^{d},\mathbb{R})\cap S^{\alpha_{0}}([0,1]^{d},\mathbb{R})
for some
\alpha_{0}>0
,
\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}||b_{0,\theta}||_{\alpha_{0}}<\infty
, and
\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}||b_{0,\theta}||_{2,2,\alpha_{0}}<\infty
. Then, Lemma 3 of
van der Vaart and van Zanten (2011)
and Lemma 13 in
Walker (2026a)
imply that the
h_{n,2}
rate is
\delta_{n}=n^{-\min\{\alpha,\alpha_{0}\}/(2\alpha+d)}
. Consequently,
\delta_{n}=o(n^{-1/4})
iff
\alpha>d/2
and
\alpha_{0}>\alpha/2+d/4
. The second inequality restricts oversmoothing because, if
\alpha\leq\alpha_{0}
(i.e., undersmoothing), the contraction rate is
\delta_{n}=n^{-\alpha/(2\alpha+d)}
and
\delta_{n}=o(n^{-1/4})
if
\alpha>d/2
. If
\alpha=\alpha_{0}
, then
\delta_{n}
is the minimax rate
n^{-\alpha_{0}/(2\alpha_{0}+d)}
and
\delta_{n}=o(n^{-1/4})
if
\alpha_{0}>d/2
. Substituting
\delta_{n}=n^{-\min\{\alpha,\alpha_{0}\}/(2\alpha+d)}
into the
h_{n,\infty}
rate
\tilde{\delta}_{n}
, one can show
\tilde{\delta}_{n}=o(n^{-1/4})
if
\alpha/2+3d_{z}/4<\alpha_{0}
and
1/4+(d_{z}+(\alpha-a))/(2\alpha+d_{z})<\min\{\alpha,\alpha_{0}\}/(2\alpha+d)
.
27
27

27

See the discussion following Lemma 13 in
Walker (2026a)
for more details on the rate calculations.

5.3.
Verifying Prior Invariance

The next proposition states that (
4
) holds if the
Chamberlain (1987)
optimal residual,
\tilde{g}_{0}(w,z,\gamma_{0})=M_{0}(z,\gamma_{0})^{\prime}\Sigma_{0}^{-1}(z,\gamma_{0})g(w,z,\gamma_{0})
, is well-approximated by sequences in
\mathcal{H}
. Let
\tilde{g}_{0,\theta}(u,z,\gamma_{0})=\tilde{g}_{0}(F_{\theta,z}^{-1}(u),z,\gamma_{0}),z,\gamma_{0})
for
(u,z,\theta)\in[0,1]^{d_{w}}\times[0,1]^{d_{z}}\times\Theta
be the optimal residual transformed so that is defined on
[0,1]^{d}
.

Proposition 3
.

Suppose Assumptions
4
,
5
,
11
,
12
,
13
,
14
, and the conditions of Proposition
1
hold. Further, suppose there are sequences
\{\tilde{g}_{n,\theta,j}^{*}:\theta\in\Theta\}_{n\geq 1}
,
j=1,...,d_{\gamma},
in
\mathcal{H}
and
\{\zeta_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
in
\mathbb{R}_{++}
with
\zeta_{n}\rightarrow 0
such that 1.
\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\max_{1\leq j\leq d_{\gamma}}||\tilde{g}_{n,\theta,j}^{*}-\tilde{g}_{0,\theta,j}||_{\infty}\lesssim\zeta_{n}
, 2.
\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\max_{1\leq j\leq d_{\gamma}}||\tilde{g}_{n,\theta,j}^{*}||_{\mathcal{H}}\lesssim\sqrt{n}\zeta_{n}
, 3.
\sqrt{n}\zeta_{n}\delta_{n}\rightarrow 0
as
n\rightarrow\infty
, where
\delta_{n}
is the RMSH rate, and 4.
\{\Delta_{n,\theta}^{*}:\theta\in\Theta\}
with
\Delta_{n,\theta}^{*}(w,z)=\tilde{g}_{n,\theta}^{*}(F_{\theta,z}(w),z)-\tilde{g}_{0,\theta}(F_{\theta,z}(w),z)
for
(w,z)\in\mathcal{W}\times[0,1]^{d_{z}}
satisfies, for
P_{0,Z}^{\infty}
-almost every fixed realization
\{z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
of
\{Z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
,

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\left|\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{\Delta_{n,\theta}^{*}(W_{i},z_{i})-E_{P_{0,W|Z}}[\Delta_{n,\theta}^{*}(W,Z)|Z=z_{i}]\}\right|\right|_{2}\overset{P_{0,W|Z}^{(n)}}{\longrightarrow}0

as
n\rightarrow\infty
. Then, for
P_{0,Z}^{\infty}
-almost every fixed realization
\{z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
of
\{Z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
, there is
\{\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}\}_{n\geq 1}
such that
\Pi(p_{W|Z}\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}|W^{(n)})\rightarrow 1
in
P_{0,W|Z}^{(n)}
-probability and (
4
) holds.

Some comments on Proposition
3
. Sequences satisfying 1. and 2. can be found by solving
\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\inf_{f\in\mathcal{H}:||f-\tilde{g}_{0,\theta,j}||_{\infty}\leq\zeta_{n,j}}\frac{1}{2}||f||_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\leq n\zeta_{n,j}^{2}
for
j=1,...,d_{\gamma}
and setting
\zeta_{n}=\max_{1\leq j\leq d_{\gamma}}\zeta_{n,j}
. Since LHS is the component of the concentration function that describes the approximation of
\tilde{g}_{0,\theta,j}
by
\mathcal{H}
, this can be checked for many GPs
(
van der Vaart and van Zanten, 2008a
;
van der Vaart and van Zanten, 2008b
;
van der Vaart and van Zanten, 2011
)
. The condition
\sqrt{n}\zeta_{n}\delta_{n}\rightarrow 0
as
n\rightarrow\infty
in 3. is known as a ‘no-bias’ condition, and, since
\delta_{n}=o(n^{-1/4})
, it holds if
\zeta_{n}=o(n^{-1/4})
.
28
28

28

This label follows
Castillo (2024)
;
Castillo (2012)
,
Rivoirard and Rousseau (2012a)
, and
Castillo and Rousseau (2015)
, where similar conditions are likened to frequentist ‘no-bias’ conditions (e.g., Section 25.8 of
van der Vaart (1998)
).
Example
2
below demonstrates that this restricts the smoothness of
B
relative to
\tilde{g}_{0,\theta}
. Part 4. amounts to a complexity constraint on
\{\Delta_{n,\theta}^{*}:\theta\in\Theta\}
because
\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}||\Delta_{n,\theta}^{*}||_{\infty}=o(1)
implies that the convergence holds pointwise. Finally, if
\{g_{0,\theta,j}:\theta\in\Theta\}\subseteq\mathcal{H}
for each
j
, then Proposition
3
holds trivially (i.e.,
g_{n,\theta,j}^{*}=\tilde{g}_{0,\theta,j}
and
\zeta_{n,j}=0
for each
j
), however, for many GPs, the RKHS comprises a relatively small class of functions.

Example 2

(Continued)
.

Suppose that
\{\tilde{g}_{0,\theta,j}:\theta\in\Theta\}\subseteq C^{\beta_{0,j}}(T,\mathbb{R})\cap H^{\beta_{0,j}}([0,1]^{d},\mathbb{R})
for some
\beta_{0,1},...,\beta_{0,d_{\gamma}}>0
, and
\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}||\tilde{g}_{0,\theta,j}||_{\beta_{0,j}}<\infty
and
\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}||\tilde{g}_{0,\theta,j}||_{2,2,\beta_{0,j}}<\infty
for
j=1,...,d_{\gamma}
. If
\beta_{0,j}<\alpha
for some
j
, then Lemma 13 in
Walker (2026a)
yields that
\zeta_{n}=n^{-\underline{\beta}/(2\alpha+d)}
, where
\underline{\beta}=\min_{1\leq j\leq d_{\gamma}}\beta_{0,j}
, and
\zeta_{n}=o(n^{-1/4})
if
\underline{\beta}>\alpha/2+d/4
. If
\alpha+d/2>\underline{\beta}>\alpha
, then
\zeta_{n}=o(n^{-1/4})
holds as long as
\alpha>d/2
. Finally, if
\underline{\beta}>\alpha+d/2
, then
\{\tilde{g}_{0,\theta,j}:\theta\in\Theta\}\subseteq\mathcal{H}
for all
j
and Proposition
3
holds trivially.

6.
Conclusion and Extensions

This paper proposes semiparametric Bayesian inference for conditional moment equalities. The central idea is that a posterior for a conditional distribution of data implies a posterior for a minimum distance estimand based on the conditional moments. The framework has similar flexibility to frequentist semiparametric estimators, and does not require converting the conditional moments to unconditional moments. I also establish the method’s frequentist optimality via a BvM, providing conditions under which the posterior is asymptotically equivalent to a
Chamberlain (1987)
efficient estimator.

My paper offers several directions for future research. First, there are important settings in which
\gamma
is infinite-dimensional. An example is nonparametric IV in which
W=(Y,D)^{\prime}
,
Z=(X,A)^{\prime}
, and
g(W,Z,\gamma)=Y-\gamma(D,X)
, where
\gamma(\cdot,\cdot)
is an unknown function
(
Ai and Chen, 2003
;
Newey and Powell, 2024
)
. Conceptually, the same ideas apply: a posterior for
P_{W|Z}
implies a posterior for a
function-valued

\gamma
. However, theoretical issues, such as non-compact function spaces, ill-posedness of identifying conditions, etc., may warrant different minimum distance estimands (e.g., penalized estimands like in
Chen and Pouzo (2012)
).

Second, model misspecification is an important concern for conditional moment equality models (i.e., when
E_{P_{W|Z}}[g(W,Z,\gamma)|Z]\neq 0
for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
, with positive probability). The posterior for the value function
Q_{n}(\gamma_{n},\gamma_{n},p_{W|Z})
, a ‘
J
-statistic’-like estimand, contains information about the compatibility of the conditional moments with the data. Developing a Bayesian specification assessment framework using this posterior and comparing it with classical overidentifying restrictions tests would be interesting. Moreover, reporting the posterior distribution of
Q_{n}(\gamma_{n},\gamma_{n},p_{W|Z})
may also connect with the recommendations in
Andrews et al. (2025)
of reporting
J
-statistics (not
J
-tests) in overidentified models.

Finally, competing structural models often lead to nonnested conditional moment equalities
E_{P_{W|Z}}[g_{1}(W,Z,\gamma_{1})|Z]=0
and
E_{P_{W|Z}}[g_{2}(W,Z,\gamma_{2})|Z]=0
. A posterior for
p_{W|Z}
leads to a joint posterior for
(\gamma_{n,1},Q_{n,1},\gamma_{n,2},Q_{n,2})
, where
Q_{n,j}
,
j\in\{1,2\}
, is shorthand for
Q_{n,j}(\gamma_{n,j},\gamma_{n,j},p_{W|Z})
, with
Q_{n,j}(\gamma,\tilde{\gamma},p_{W|Z})
being (
1
) for
E_{P_{W|Z}}[g_{j}(W,Z,\gamma_{j})|Z]
. The posterior for
Q_{n,1}-Q_{n,2}
can be used to assess which conditional moments are most plausible.

Appendix A
Existence and Measurability of
\gamma_{n}

I state sufficient conditions for Assumption
3
. A discussion follows.

Assumption 15
.

The following holds for almost every realization
\{z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
of
\{Z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
and every
n\geq 1
, 1.
\Gamma
is a compact subset of
(\mathbb{R}^{d_{\gamma}},||\cdot||_{2})
, 2.
p_{W|Z}\mapsto Q_{n}(\gamma,\tilde{\gamma},p_{W|Z})
is
\mathscr{P}_{W|Z}
-measurable for each
(\gamma,\tilde{\gamma})\in\Gamma\times\Gamma
, 3.
\gamma\mapsto Q_{n}(\gamma,\tilde{\gamma},p_{W|Z})
is continuous for every
\tilde{\gamma}\in\Gamma
and every
p_{W|Z}\in\mathcal{P}_{W|Z}
, and 4.
q_{n}(\tilde{\gamma},p_{W|Z})=\argmin_{\gamma\in\Gamma}Q_{n}(\gamma,\tilde{\gamma},p_{W|Z})
is unique and satisfies
q_{n}(\tilde{\gamma},p_{W|Z})\in\text{int}(\Gamma)
for every
\tilde{\gamma}\in\Gamma
and every
p_{W|Z}\in\mathcal{P}_{W|Z}
.

Assumption 16
.

The following holds for almost every realization
\{z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
of
\{Z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
and every
n\geq 1
: 1.
\gamma\mapsto m(z_{i},\gamma)
,
i=1,...,n
, is twice continuously differentiable for each
p_{W|Z}\in\mathcal{P}_{W|Z}
, 2.
\gamma\mapsto\Sigma(z_{i},\gamma)
,
i=1,...,n
, is continuously differentiable for each
p_{W|Z}\in\mathcal{P}_{W|Z}
, 3.
\inf_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\min_{1\leq i\leq n}\lambda_{min}(\Sigma(z_{i},\gamma))>0
for each
p_{W|Z}\in\mathcal{P}_{W|Z}
, and 4.
(x_{1},x_{2})\mapsto Q_{n}(x_{1},x_{2},p_{W|Z})
satisfies

\displaystyle\sup_{\tilde{\gamma}\in\Gamma}\left|\left|\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x_{1}\partial x_{2}^{\prime}}Q_{n}(q_{n}(\tilde{\gamma},p_{W|Z}),\tilde{\gamma},p_{W|Z})\right|\right|_{op}<\inf_{\tilde{\gamma}\in\Gamma}\lambda_{min}\left(\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x_{1}\partial x_{1}^{\prime}}Q_{n}(q_{n}(\tilde{\gamma},p_{W|Z}),\tilde{\gamma},p_{W|Z})\right)

for every
p_{W|Z}\in\mathcal{P}_{W|Z}
.

Assumptions
15
and
16
are similar to Assumption 1 in
Hansen and Lee (2021)
, except that they apply to
q_{n}(\tilde{\gamma},p_{W|Z})
rather than a GMM estimand. The key condition is Assumption
16
.4, which requires that
n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}M(z_{i},q_{n}(\tilde{\gamma},p_{W|Z}))^{\prime}\Sigma^{-1}(z_{i},\tilde{\gamma})m(z_{i},q_{n}(\tilde{\gamma},p_{W|Z}))
not deviate too far from zero under small changes to
\Sigma^{-1}(z_{i},\tilde{\gamma})
(with deviations constrained by how strongly identified
q_{n}(\tilde{\gamma},p_{W|Z})
is). Combined with the other assumptions, the condition implies
\tilde{\gamma}\mapsto q_{n}(\tilde{\gamma},p_{W|Z})
is a measurable contraction mapping. Consequently, a probabilistic analogue to the Banach Fixed Point Theorem
(
Bharucha-Reid, 1976
)
reveals that
\tilde{\gamma}\mapsto q_{n}(\tilde{\gamma},p_{W|Z})
has a unique fixed point
\gamma_{n}
for each
p_{W|Z}
that can be characterized as the
r\rightarrow\infty
limit of the recursive relation
\gamma_{n,r}=q_{n}(\gamma_{n,r-1},p_{W|Z})
(and
p_{W|Z}\mapsto\gamma_{n}
is measurable). This discussion is summarized in the next proposition, and, since the result concerns a conditional minimum distance estimand, it may be of independent interest.

Proposition 4
.

If Assumptions
15
and
16
hold, then Assumption
3
holds.

Appendix B
Implementation Details and Data-Calibrated Simulation

B.1.
Sampling from the Posterior for the Conditional Density

B.1.1.
Covariance Function and Base Density

The Matérn covariance function is computed using the formula
\kappa(t,s)=\Gamma^{-1}(\alpha)2^{1-\alpha}(\sqrt{2\alpha}||t-s||_{2})^{\alpha}K_{\alpha}(\sqrt{2\alpha}||t-s||_{2})
for
t,s\in[0,1]^{d}
, where
\Gamma(\cdot)
is the gamma function,
K_{\alpha}
is the modified Bessel function of the second kind of order
\alpha
, and
\alpha=5/2
(following
Rasmussen and Williams (2006)
). I specify
f_{\theta}(w|z)
to be the density of
\mathcal{N}(\mu_{q}^{\prime}\tilde{Z},\xi_{q}^{2})\otimes\mathcal{N}(\mu_{p}^{\prime}Z,\xi_{p}^{2})
, where
\tilde{Z}=(Z^{\prime},\log P)^{\prime}
and, for this case only,
Z=(1,\log Y,\log A)
. This means
\theta=(\mu_{q}^{\prime},\mu_{p}^{\prime},\xi_{p}^{2},\xi_{q}^{2})^{\prime}
. I set
\xi_{q}^{2},\xi_{p}^{2}\overset{iid}{\sim}InvGamma(0.001,0.001)
and
\mu_{j}|\xi_{p}^{2},\xi_{q}^{2}\overset{ind}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(0,10^{3}\cdot\xi_{j}^{2})
for each
j\in\{p,q\}
. Finally,
F_{0}(z_{i})=(F_{0,1}(z_{i,1}),F_{0,2}(z_{i,2}))^{\prime}
, where
F_{0,j}(z_{i,j})=(z_{i,j}-\underline{z}_{n,j})/(\overline{z}_{n,j}-\underline{z}_{n,j})
,
z_{i,1}=\log y_{i}
,
z_{i,2}=\log a_{i}
,
\overline{z}_{n,j}=\max_{1\leq i\leq n}z_{i,j}
, and
\underline{z}_{n,j}=\min_{1\leq i\leq n}z_{i,j}
.

B.1.2.
Sampling from the Posterior for
p_{\theta,B}

I follow
Tokdar (2007)
and
Tokdar et al. (2010)
, replacing
B
with the
kriging

B^{*}
, where
B^{*}(t)=E[B(t)|B(t_{1}),...,B(t_{K})]
,
t\in[0,1]^{d}
, for a prespecified set of nodes
t_{1},...,t_{K}\in[0,1]^{d}
.
29
29

29

The reason for kriging is that the normalizing integral in
p_{\theta,B}(\cdot|z)
depends on the entire function
B(\cdot,F_{0}(z)):[0,1]^{d_{w}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, which complicates likelihood evaluation. It is an approximation because if the nodes grow dense in
[0,1]^{d}
as
K\rightarrow\infty
then
B^{*}
converges to
B

(
Ghosal and Van der Vaart, 2017
)

B^{*}
is also a centered GP satisfying
B^{*}(t)=\lambda^{\prime}\mathcal{K}^{-1/2}\kappa_{1:K}(t)
, where
\lambda\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I)
,
\mathcal{K}=(\kappa(t_{k},t_{j}))_{k,j=1}^{K}
, and
\kappa_{1:K}(t)=(\kappa(t_{1},t),...,\kappa(t_{K},t))^{\prime}
, and leads to a Metropolis-within-Gibbs algorithm to sample from

\displaystyle\pi(\lambda,\theta|W^{(n)})\propto\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left\{f_{\theta}(W_{i}|z_{i})\frac{\exp(B^{*}(F_{\theta,z_{i}}(W_{i}),F_{0}(z_{i})))}{\int_{[0,1]^{d_{w}}}\exp(B^{*}(u,F_{0}(z_{i})))du}\right\}p_{\mathcal{N}(0,I)}(\lambda)\pi_{\Theta}(\theta),

where
\pi_{\Theta}
is the density of
\Pi_{\Theta}
. Specifically, given an initial parameter value
(\theta_{old}^{\prime},\lambda_{old}^{\prime})^{\prime}
, a sweep of the Gibbs sampling algorithm applies the following steps:

1.

Sample
\lambda_{new}\sim\Pi(\lambda\in\cdot|W^{(n)},\theta_{old})
using Metropolis-Hastings (MH) with proposal
\mathcal{N}(\lambda_{old},c^{2}I)
.

2.

Sample
\theta_{new}\sim\Pi(\theta\in\cdot|W^{(n)},\lambda_{new})
using MH with proposal density
q_{n}(\theta|\theta_{old})\propto\prod_{i=1}^{n}f_{\theta}(W_{i}|z_{i})\pi_{\Theta}(\theta)
.

Iterating over these steps
S
times leads to a sample
\{(\lambda^{[s]},\theta^{[s]})\}_{s=1}^{S}
from
\pi(\lambda,\theta|W^{(n)})
. In terms of specific implementation: 1. I adapt
c>0
during the burn-in to target an acceptance probability of 35%, 2. sampling from
q_{n}(\cdot|\theta_{old})
exploits conjugacy of the normal-inverse gamma prior in Gaussian linear regression and conforms to a recommendation in
Chib and Greenberg (1995)
, and 3. I set
\{t_{k}\}_{k=1}^{K}=(\{j/2\}_{j=0}^{2})^{2}\times\{\hat{F}_{0,n,j}\}_{j=1}^{3}
, where
\hat{F}_{0,n,j}
are
k
-means cluster centers of
\{F_{0}(z_{i})\}_{j=1}^{n}
, setting the number of clusters equal to
3
.
30
30

30

The choice
(\{j/2\}_{j=0}^{2})^{2}
for
W
corresponds to a node configuration in
Tokdar (2007)
, while
\{\hat{z}_{n,j}\}_{j=1}^{3}
reflects that
\prod_{i=1}^{n}p_{\theta,B}(W_{i}|z_{i})
only depends on
\{B(\cdot,z_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
thereby serving as parsimonious approximation to using
\{z_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
as nodes for
z
, an alternative that eliminates node selection for
z
but increases the number of parameters to
9n
.
There are data-driven node selection schemes
(
Tokdar, 2007
;
Tokdar et al., 2010
)
, however these can be computationally intensive. Given
\{(B^{*,[s]},\theta^{[s]})\}_{s=1}^{S}
(with
B^{*,[s]}(t)=\lambda^{[s]^{\prime}}\mathcal{K}^{-1/2}\kappa_{1:K}(t)
), I obtain
\{\gamma_{n}^{[s]}\}_{s=1}^{S}
using the importance sampling algorithm described in Section
2.2
.

Remark 5
.

Kriging is introduced purely for computation. The asymptotic analysis in Section
5
is based on the underlying
B
, which is consistent with standard theoretical treatment of GP
density estimation
(
Tokdar and Ghosh, 2007
;
van der Vaart and van Zanten, 2008a
;
Tokdar et al., 2010
;
Castillo and Rousseau, 2015
)
. This distinction is important because if advances in Bayesian computation make it possible bypass kriging (and use
B
), then the asymptotic theory still applies.
31
31

31

Note that, since
B^{*}
is a centered GP, conditions for Propositions
1
–
3
could be derived for this class (i.e., an exercise similar to Example
2
, except with covariance function
\kappa(t,s)=\kappa_{1:K}(t)^{\prime}\mathcal{K}^{-1}\kappa_{1:K}(s)
).

B.2.
Data-Calibrated Simulation

B.2.1.
Simulation DGP and Target Parameter

I calibrate the simulation DGP to the 2001 National Household Travel Survey gasoline demand dataset. Specifically, I simulate:

(5)

\displaystyle\log Q

\displaystyle=\gamma_{0}+\gamma_{1}\log P+\gamma_{2}\log Y+U_{1},\quad E[U_{1}|\log Y,\log A]=0

(6)

\displaystyle\log P

\displaystyle=\lambda_{0}+\lambda_{1}\log Y+\lambda_{2}\log A+U_{2},\quad E[U_{2}|\log Y,\log A]=0,

where
(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})=(4.413,-1.631,0.293)
and
(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})=(0.178,-0.001,0.066)
. The values of
\gamma
and
\lambda
are the Wald estimates based on
E[U_{1}(1,\log Y,\log A)]=0
and the OLS estimates of (
6
), respectively, using the full dataset. The target parameter is deadweight loss (DWL) from a price change from
p^{0}=\$1.22
and
p^{1}=\$1.44
for
y\in\{\$42500,\$72500\}
(resulting in DWL of
\$33.05
and
\$38.74
for the low- and high-income groups, respectively). Samples
\{(A_{i_{j}}^{*},Y_{i_{j}}^{*},Q_{i_{j}}^{*},P_{i_{j}}^{*})\}_{j=1}^{n}
are generated as follows:

1.

Sample indices
\{i_{1},...,i_{n}\}
by sampling from
\{1,...,4812\}
with replacement.

2.

Sample
(\varepsilon_{i_{1},1},\varepsilon_{i_{1},2},\varepsilon_{i_{1},3},\varepsilon_{i_{1},4}),...,(\varepsilon_{i_{n},1},\varepsilon_{i_{n},2},\varepsilon_{i_{n},3},\varepsilon_{i_{n},4})
independently from
\mathcal{N}(0,\widehat{\Omega})
, where
\widehat{\Omega}=\frac{1}{100}\text{diag}(\widehat{Var}(\log Y),\widehat{Var}(\log A),\widehat{Var}(\log P),\widehat{Var}(\log Q))
with
\widehat{Var}
denoting the sample variance.

3.

Generate
\log Y_{i_{j}}^{*}=\log Y_{i_{j}}+\varepsilon_{i_{j},1}
and
\log A_{i_{j}}^{*}=\log A_{i_{j}}+\varepsilon_{i_{j},2}
for
j=1,...,n
.

4.

Sample
R_{i_{1}},...,R_{i_{n}}
independently from a Rademacher distribution.

5.

Generate
\log P_{i_{j}}^{*}=\lambda_{0}+\lambda_{1}\log Y_{i_{j}}^{*}+\lambda_{2}\log A_{i_{j}}^{*}+R_{i_{j}}U_{i_{j},2}+\varepsilon_{i_{j},3}
for
j=1,...,n
, where
U_{i,2}
are the OLS residuals from the full dataset.

6.

Generate
\log Q_{i_{j}}^{*}=\gamma_{0}+\gamma_{1}\log P_{i_{j}}^{*}+\gamma_{2}\log Y_{i_{j}}^{*}+R_{i_{j}}U_{i_{j},1}+\varepsilon_{i_{j},4}
for
j=1,...,n
, where
U_{i,1}
are the IV/Wald residuals from the full dataset.

Adding elements of
(\varepsilon_{i_{j},1},\varepsilon_{i_{j},2},\varepsilon_{i_{j},3},\varepsilon_{i_{j},4})
guarantees all variables are continuously distributed. Interacting
R_{i_{j}}
and
U_{i_{j},k}
,
k\in\{1,2\}
, ensures that the conditional moment restrictions hold at
\gamma
and allows for heteroskedasticity of an unknown form. Multiplying
U_{i_{j},1}
and
U_{i_{j},2}
by a common
R_{i_{j}}
preserves the first- and second-stage residual covariance.

B.2.2.
Other Procedures

I implement semiparametric Bayes (SB) by running
S=50,000
iterations of the Metropolis-Within-Gibbs sampler, discarding the first half as burn-in, and keeping every fifth thereafter (resulting in
5,001
draws from the posterior). I compare with:

1.

Two-Stage Least Squares (TSLS).

\hat{\gamma}_{TSLS}=(\sum_{i=1}^{n}z_{i}X_{i}^{\prime})^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}z_{i}\log Q_{i}
, where
X_{i}=(1,\log P_{i},\log Y_{i})^{\prime}
and
z_{i}=(1,\log y_{i},\log a_{i})^{\prime}
. The estimator for the asymptotic variance is
\hat{V}_{TSLS}=n\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}z_{i}X_{i}^{\prime}\right)^{-1}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}z_{i}z_{i}^{\prime}\hat{U}_{i}^{2}\right)\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}z_{i}^{\prime}\right)^{-1},
where
\hat{U}_{i}=\log Q_{i}-\hat{\gamma}^{\prime}X_{i}
. Standard errors for DWL are based on the delta method.

2.

Bayesian Bootstrap (BB).
BB is a weighted bootstrap: for each repetition, sample
V_{1},...,V_{n}\overset{iid}{\sim}Expo(1)
and compute
\gamma_{BB}=(\sum_{i=1}^{n}V_{i}z_{i}X_{i}^{\prime})^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}V_{i}z_{i}\log Q_{i}
. The output of
S
bootstrap repetitions
\{\gamma^{[s]}_{BB}\}_{s=1}^{S}
is a sample from the BB posterior for
\gamma
, and
\{DWL(\gamma^{[s]}_{BB},p^{0},p^{1},y)\}_{s=1}^{S}
is a sample from the BB DWL posterior. Bayesian bootstrapping the Wald estimand conforms to the IV example in
Chamberlain and Imbens (2003)
. The BB posterior median is the point estimator for DWL, the DWL credible interval is the BB 95% equitailed probability interval, and I set
S=5,001
.

3.

Plug-In Efficient (PE).
The PE estimator is computed as follows: 1. calculate
\hat{\gamma}_{TSLS}
, and estimate
E[\log P|Z=z_{i}]
and
E[U^{2}|Z=z_{i}]
using the fitted values from series regressions of
\log P
and
\hat{U}^{2}
, where
\hat{U}=\log Q-\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}Z
, on
Z
, respectively, and 2. compute
\hat{\gamma}_{PE}=(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\hat{\omega}(z_{i},\tilde{\gamma})\hat{M}(z_{i})X_{i}^{\prime})^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\hat{\omega}(z_{i},\tilde{\gamma})\hat{M}(z_{i})\log Q_{i}
, where
\hat{M}(z)=(1,\hat{E}[\log P|Z=z],\log Y)^{\prime}
and
\hat{\omega}(z,\gamma)=1/\hat{E}[U^{2}|Z=z]
with
\hat{E}[U^{2}|Z]
being the series regression estimator of
E[U^{2}|Z]
.
32
32

32

Due to divide-by-zero issues,
\hat{E}[U^{2}|Z]
is computed as
\max\{\hat{E}[U^{2}|Z],10^{-3}\}
for the PE estimator.
The plug-in estimator of the
Chamberlain (1987)
asymptotic variance is computed using the formula
\hat{V}_{PE}=(n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\hat{\omega}(z_{i},\tilde{\gamma})\hat{M}(z_{i})\hat{M}(z_{i})^{\prime})^{-1}
and standard errors for the DWL are based on the delta method. I use the polynomial basis
\{(\log y)^{j}(\log a)^{k}\}_{j,k=0}^{J}
with ad hoc choices
J=2
for
n=1,000
and
J=3
for
n=2,500
.

4.

Bayesian ETEL (BETEL).
The BETEL posterior satisfies

\displaystyle\pi(\gamma|W^{(n)})\propto\prod_{i=1}^{n}\hat{p}_{i}(\gamma)\pi_{\Gamma}(\gamma)\mathbf{1}\{\gamma\in\text{int}(Co(\gamma))\},

where

\displaystyle\hat{p}_{i}(\gamma)=\frac{\exp(\hat{\lambda}(\gamma)^{\prime}g(W_{i},z_{i},\gamma)h(z_{i}))}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}\exp(\hat{\lambda}(\gamma)^{\prime}g(W_{j},z_{j},\gamma)h(z_{j}))},

for each
i=1,...,n
,
\hat{\lambda}(\gamma)=\argmin_{\gamma\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{h}}}n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\exp(\lambda^{\prime}g(W_{i},z_{i},\gamma)h(z_{i}))
,
h(z)
is a
d_{h}\times 1
vector of transformations of
z
,
\pi_{\Gamma}
is a prior for
\gamma
, and
Co(\gamma)
is the convex hull of
\{g(W_{i},z_{i},\gamma)h(z_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
. I set
h(z)
to the natural cubic spline basis from p. 744 of
Chib et al. (2022)
with their recommended choice of
\lfloor 2\cdot n^{1/6}\rfloor
knots, and use the
Chib et al. (2018)
;
Chib et al. (2022)
default Student’s
t
prior for
\gamma
in both cases. I use the posterior median as the point estimator and the 95% equitailed probability interval to quantify uncertainty about DWL. Results are based on
50,000
MH draws, discarding the first half as burn-in, and saving every fifth thereafter.

B.2.3.
Simulation Results

Table
2
reports the simulation results based on
500
independent datasets. The MAE and Bias columns report the median absolute error (MAE) and median bias (Bias), respectively, of the point estimators.
33
33

33

I report MAE instead of mean-squared error (and median bias instead of mean bias) because TSLS has no moments in the just-identified case with continuous data.
SB has noticeably smaller MAE when
n=1,000
. When
n=2,500
, SB has the smallest MAE among procedures that estimate the
Chamberlain (1987)
optimal IVs (i.e., SB, PE, and BETEL), and is comparable to TSLS and BB. Regarding uncertainty quantification, when
n=1,000
, the procedures based on the optimal IVs exhibit more severe (frequentist) undercoverage than BB and TSLS. However, when
n=2,500
, SB achieves frequentist coverage close to the nominal level of 95%, and, across all of the procedures, SB credible intervals have the shortest length. Although specific to one DGP, these results are evidence that SB has solid finite-sample frequentist performance in an empirically relevant setting (i.e., inference for counterfactuals).

Appendix C
Proofs of Theorems and Corollaries

C.1.
Proof of Theorem
1

I state technical lemmas. Proofs are in
Walker (2026a)
.

Lemma 1
.

Suppose Assumptions
7
.1 and
10
.3 hold. Then, for
P_{0,Z}^{\infty}
-almost every fixed realization
\{z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
of
\{Z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
,

\displaystyle\sup_{(\gamma,p_{W|Z})\in\Gamma\times\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}}\max_{1\leq i\leq n}||\Sigma^{-1}(z_{i},\gamma)||_{op}=O(1),

where
\{\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}\}_{n\geq 1}
are the sets introduced in Assumption
10
.

Lemma 2
.

Suppose that Assumptions
4
,
5
,
6
.1,
7
.1,
8
,
10
.1, and
10
.3 hold. Then, for
P_{0,Z}^{\infty}
-almost every fixed realization
\{z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
of
\{Z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
,

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\sup_{(\tilde{\gamma},p_{W|Z})\in\Gamma\times\mathcal{P}_{n,W|Z}}||q_{n}(\tilde{\gamma},p_{W|Z})-\gamma_{0}||_{2}=0

where
\{\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}\}_{n\geq 1}
are the sets defined in Assumption
10
.

Lemma 3
.

If Assumptions
4
,
5
,
6
,
7
.1,
8
,
9
, and
10
hold, then, for
P_{0,Z}^{\infty}
-almost every fixed realization
\{z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
of
\{Z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
and
n
large, the function
\tilde{\gamma}\mapsto q_{n}(\tilde{\gamma},p_{W|Z})
is a uniform contraction mapping over
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}
. That is, there exists a constant
\rho\in[0,1)
and
N\geq 1
such that
||q_{n}(\tilde{\gamma}_{1},p_{W|Z})-q_{n}(\tilde{\gamma}_{2},p_{W|Z})||_{2}\leq\rho||\tilde{\gamma}_{1}-\tilde{\gamma}_{2}||_{2}
for all
\tilde{\gamma}_{1},\tilde{\gamma}_{2}\in\Gamma
, all
p_{W|Z}\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}
, and
n\geq N
.

Lemma 4
.

If Assumptions
3
,
4
,

,
6
,

.1,
8
,
9
, and
10
hold, then, for
P_{0,Z}^{\infty}
-almost every fixed realization
\{z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
of
\{Z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
,

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\sup_{p_{W|Z}\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}}||\gamma_{n}-\gamma_{0}||_{2}=0,

where
\{\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}\}_{n\geq 1}
are the sets defined in Assumption

.

Lemma 5
.

Suppose that Assumptions
3
,
4
,
5
,
6
,
7
,
8
,
9
, and
10
hold. Then, for
P_{0,Z}^{\infty}
-almost every realization
\{z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
of
\{Z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
,

\displaystyle\sup_{p_{W|Z}\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}}\left|\left|M(\cdot,\gamma_{n})-M_{0}(\cdot,\gamma_{0})\right|\right|_{n,2}\lesssim o(n^{-1/4})+\sup_{p_{W|Z}\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}}||\gamma_{n}-\gamma_{0}||_{2}

and

\displaystyle\sup_{p_{W|Z}\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}}\left|\left|\Sigma(\cdot,\gamma_{n})-\Sigma_{0}(\cdot,\gamma_{0})\right|\right|_{n,\infty}\lesssim o(n^{-1/4})+\sup_{p_{W|Z}\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}}||\gamma_{n}-\gamma_{0}||_{2}

where

.

Lemma 6
.

Let
V_{n}(\gamma,\bar{\gamma},p_{W|Z})=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}M(z_{i},\gamma)^{\prime}\Sigma^{-1}(z_{i},\gamma)M(z_{i},\bar{\gamma})
. If Assumptions
3
,
4
,
5
,
6
,
7
,
8
,
9
, and
10
hold, then, for
P_{0,Z}^{\infty}
-almost every fixed realization
\{z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
of
\{Z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
and for every positive sequence
\{r_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
with
r_{n}\rightarrow 0
as
n\rightarrow\infty
, the collection of matrices
\{V_{n}(\gamma,\bar{\gamma},p_{W|Z}):(\gamma,\bar{\gamma},p_{W|Z})\in B(0,r_{n})\times B(0,r_{n})\times\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}\}
forms a class of nonsingular matrices for
n
large, where
\{\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}\}_{n\geq 1}
are defined in Assumption
10
and
\{B(\gamma_{0},r_{n})\}_{n\geq 1}
are open balls in
(\mathbb{R}^{d_{\gamma}},||\cdot||_{2})
centered at
\gamma_{0}
and with radii
\{r_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
. Moreover,
\sup_{(\gamma,\bar{\gamma},p_{W|Z})\in B(\gamma_{0},r_{n})\times B(\gamma_{0},r_{n})\times\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}}||V_{n}^{-1}(\gamma,\bar{\gamma},p_{W|Z})||_{op}=O(1)
.

Lemma 7
.

Suppose that Assumptions
3
,
4
,
5
,
6
,
7
,
8
,
9
, and
10
.1–
10
.5 hold. Then, for
P_{0,Z}^{\infty}
-almost every fixed realization
\{z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
of
\{Z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
, the following holds:

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\sup_{p_{W|Z}\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}}||n^{\frac{1}{4}}(\gamma_{n}-\gamma_{0})||_{2}=0,

where
\{\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}\}_{n\geq 1}
are the sets defined in Assumption
10
.

Proof of Theorem
1
.

The proof has five steps. Step 1 shows that the lemma holds if four conditions are met. Steps 2–5 are verify these conditions in order to prove the theorem.

Step 1.

Let
\{z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
be an arbitrary fixed realization of
\{Z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
. Since
\gamma_{n}
satisfies the first order conditions

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}M(z_{i},\gamma_{n})^{\prime}\Sigma(z_{i},\gamma_{n})^{-1}m(z_{i},\gamma_{n})=0

and
\gamma_{n}\in\Gamma_{0}
for
n
large and for all
p_{W|Z}\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}
, I expand
m(z_{i},\gamma_{n})
around
\gamma_{0}
(using element by element mean value expansions) to obtain

\displaystyle 0=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}M(z_{i},\gamma_{n})^{\prime}\Sigma^{-1}(z_{i},\gamma_{n})m(z_{i},\gamma_{0})+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}M(z_{i},\gamma_{n})^{\prime}\Sigma^{-1}(z_{i},\gamma_{n})M(z_{i},\gamma_{n}^{\dagger})(\gamma_{n}-\gamma_{0}),

where
\gamma_{n}^{\dagger}
is on the line segment joining
\gamma_{n}
and
\gamma_{0}
(and takes different values in each row of
M(z_{i},\gamma_{n}^{\dagger})
). Lemma
4
and
6
then implies that

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}(\gamma_{n}-\gamma_{0})=-V_{n}^{-1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}M(z_{i},\gamma_{n})^{\prime}\Sigma^{-1}(z_{i},\gamma_{n})m(z_{i},\gamma_{0}).

for large
n
, where
V_{n}=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}M(z_{i},\gamma_{n})^{\prime}\Sigma^{-1}(z_{i},\gamma_{n})M(z_{i},\gamma_{n}^{\dagger})
. By definition of
\tilde{\chi}_{0}
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}E_{P_{W|Z}}[\tilde{\chi}_{0}(W,Z)|Z=z_{i}]=-V_{0}^{-1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}M_{0}(z_{i},\gamma_{0})^{\prime}\Sigma_{0}^{-1}(z_{i},\gamma_{0})m(z_{i},\gamma_{0}).

Consequently, the triangle inequality, the matrix identity
A^{-1}-B^{-1}=B^{-1}(B-A)A^{-1}
, and submultiplicativity of operator norms reveals

\displaystyle\left|\left|\sqrt{n}(\gamma_{n}-\gamma_{0})-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}E_{P_{W|Z}}[\tilde{\chi}_{0}(W,Z)|Z=z_{i}]\right|\right|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left|\left|V_{n}^{-1}\right|\right|_{op}\cdot\bigg(\left|\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\{(M(z_{i},\gamma_{n})^{\prime}\Sigma^{-1}(z_{i},\gamma_{n})-M_{0}(z_{i},\gamma_{0})^{\prime}\Sigma_{0}^{-1}(z_{i},\gamma_{0}))m(z_{i},\gamma_{0})\right\}\right|\right|_{2}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad+n^{\frac{1}{4}}||V_{n}-V_{0}||_{op}\cdot||V_{0}^{-1}||_{op}\cdot n^{\frac{1}{4}}\left|\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}M_{0}(z_{i},\gamma_{0})^{\prime}\Sigma_{0}^{-1}(z_{i},\gamma_{0})m(z_{i},\gamma_{0})\right|\right|_{2}\bigg)

for all
p_{W|Z}\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}
. This indicates that I need to show

(7)

\displaystyle\sup_{p_{W|Z}\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}}\left|\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(M(z_{i},\gamma_{n})^{\prime}\Sigma(z_{i},\gamma_{n})^{-1}-M_{0}(z_{i},\gamma_{0})^{\prime}\Sigma_{0}(z_{i},\gamma_{0})^{-1})m(z_{i},\gamma_{0})\right|\right|_{2}=o(1)

(8)

\displaystyle\sup_{p_{W|Z}\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}}n^{\frac{1}{4}}\left|\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}M_{0}(z_{i},\gamma_{0})^{\prime}\Sigma_{0}^{-1}(z_{i},\gamma_{0})m(z_{i},\gamma_{0})\right|\right|_{2}=o(1)

(9)

\displaystyle\sup_{p_{W|Z}\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}}n^{\frac{1}{4}}\left|\left|V_{n}-V_{0}\right|\right|_{op}=o(1)

(10)

\displaystyle\sup_{p_{W|Z}\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}}\left|\left|V_{n}^{-1}\right|\right|_{op}=O(1).

Step 2.

I verify (
7
). Adding/subtracting
M_{0}(z_{i},\gamma_{0})
and
\Sigma_{0}^{-1}(z_{i},\gamma_{0})
appropriately, using that
m_{0}(z_{i},\gamma_{0})=0
(Assumption
5
), and applying the triangle inequality, it suffices to show

(11)

\displaystyle\sup_{p_{W|Z}\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left|\left|(M(z_{i},\gamma_{n})-M_{0}(z_{i},\gamma_{0}))^{\prime}\Sigma^{-1}(z_{i},\gamma_{n})\left(m(z_{i},\gamma_{0})-m_{0}(z_{i},\gamma_{0})\right)\right|\right|_{2}=o(1)

and

(12)

\displaystyle\sup_{p_{W|Z}\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left|\left|M_{0}(z_{i},\gamma_{0})^{\prime}\left(\Sigma^{-1}(z_{i},\gamma_{n})-\Sigma_{0}^{-1}(z_{i},\gamma_{0})\right)\left(m(z_{i},\gamma_{0})-m_{0}(z_{i},\gamma_{0})\right)\right|\right|_{2}=o(1).

For (
11
), I use that
||Ax||_{2}\leq||A||_{op}||x||_{2}
for a matrix
A
and vector
x
to obtain

\displaystyle\left|\left|(M(z_{i},\gamma_{n})-M_{0}(z_{i},\gamma_{0}))^{\prime}\Sigma(z_{i},\gamma_{n})^{-1}\left(m(z_{i},\gamma_{0})-m_{0}(z_{i},\gamma_{0})\right)\right|\right|_{2}

\displaystyle\quad\leq||M(z_{i},\gamma_{n})-M_{0}(z_{i},\gamma_{0})||_{op}\cdot||\Sigma^{-1}(z_{i},\gamma_{n})(m(z_{i},\gamma_{0})-m_{0}(z_{i},\gamma_{0}))||_{2}

\displaystyle\quad\leq||M(z_{i},\gamma_{n})-M_{0}(z_{i},\gamma_{0})||_{op}\cdot||\Sigma^{-1}(z_{i},\gamma_{n})||_{op}\cdot||m(z_{i},\gamma_{0})-m_{0}(z_{i},\gamma_{0})||_{2}

for
i=1,...,n
and for all
n\geq 1
. Consequently, by Hölder’s inequality,

\displaystyle\sup_{p_{W|Z}\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left|\left|(M(z_{i},\gamma_{n})-M_{0}(z_{i},\gamma_{0}))^{\prime}\Sigma^{-1}(z_{i},\gamma_{n})\left(m(z_{i},\gamma_{0})-m_{0}(z_{i},\gamma_{0})\right)\right|\right|_{2}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\sup_{p_{W|Z}\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}}||\Sigma^{-1}(\cdot,\gamma_{n})||_{n,\infty}\times n^{\frac{1}{4}}\sup_{p_{W|Z}\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}}||M(\cdot,\gamma_{n})-M_{0}(\cdot,\gamma_{0})||_{n,2}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times n^{\frac{1}{4}}\sup_{p_{W|Z}\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}}||m(\cdot,\gamma_{0})-m_{0}(\cdot,\gamma_{0})||_{n,2}

\displaystyle\quad=o(1)

with the last equality holding by Lemma
1
, Assumption
10
.3(a), Lemma
5
, and Lemma
7
. For (
12
), I apply the inequality
||Ax||_{2}\leq||A||_{op}||x||_{2}
for a matrix
A
and vector
x
as well as the identity
B^{-1}-A^{-1}=A^{-1}(A-B)B^{-1}
for square matrices
A
and
B
to obtain

\displaystyle\left|\left|M_{0}(z_{i},\gamma_{0})^{\prime}\left(\Sigma^{-1}(z_{i},\gamma_{n})-\Sigma_{0}^{-1}(z_{i},\gamma_{0})\right)\left(m(z_{i},\gamma_{0})-m_{0}(z_{i},\gamma_{0})\right)\right|\right|_{2}

\displaystyle\quad\leq||M_{0}(z_{i},\gamma_{0})||_{op}\cdot||\Sigma^{-1}(z_{i},\gamma_{n})||_{op}\cdot||\Sigma(z_{i},\gamma_{n})-\Sigma_{0}(z_{i},\gamma_{0})||_{op}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times||\Sigma_{0}^{-1}(z_{i},\gamma_{0})||_{op}\cdot||m(z_{i},\gamma_{0})-m_{0}(z_{i},\gamma_{0})||_{2}.

Assumption
6
.3 and
7
implies
\sup_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}||\Sigma_{0}^{-1}(z,\gamma_{0})||_{op}<\infty
and
\sup_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}||M_{0}(z,\gamma_{0})||_{op}<\infty
. Consequently, using this result and
||Ax||_{2}\leq||A||_{op}||x||_{2}
for a matrix
A
and vector
x
,

\displaystyle\left|\left|M_{0}(z_{i},\gamma_{0})^{\prime}\left(\Sigma^{-1}(z_{i},\gamma_{n})-\Sigma_{0}^{-1}(z_{i},\gamma_{0})\right)\left(m(z_{i},\gamma_{0})-m_{0}(z_{i},\gamma_{0})\right)\right|\right|_{2}

\displaystyle\quad\leq C\cdot||\Sigma^{-1}(z_{i},\gamma_{n})||_{op}\cdot||\Sigma(z_{i},\gamma_{n})-\Sigma_{0}(z_{i},\gamma_{0})||_{op}||m(z_{i},\gamma_{0})-m_{0}(z_{i},\gamma_{0})||_{2}.

for
i=1,...,n
, where
C=\sup_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}||\Sigma_{0}^{-1}(z,\gamma_{0})||_{op}\cdot\sup_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}||M_{0}(z,\gamma_{0})||_{op}<\infty
. An application of Hölder’s inequality then implies

\displaystyle\sup_{p_{W|Z}\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left|\left|M_{0}(z_{i},\gamma_{0})^{\prime}\left(\Sigma^{-1}(z_{i},\gamma_{n})-\Sigma_{0}^{-1}(z_{i},\gamma_{0})\right)\left(m(z_{i},\gamma_{0})-m_{0}(z_{i},\gamma_{0})\right)\right|\right|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\sup_{p_{W|Z}\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}}||\Sigma^{-1}(\cdot,\gamma_{n})||_{n,\infty}\times n^{\frac{1}{4}}\sup_{p_{W|Z}\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}}||\Sigma(\cdot,\gamma_{n})-\Sigma_{0}(\cdot,\gamma_{0})||_{n,\infty}

\displaystyle\quad\times n^{\frac{1}{4}}\sup_{p_{W|Z}\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}}||m(\cdot,\gamma_{0})-m_{0}(\cdot,\gamma_{0})||_{n,2}

\displaystyle=o(1),

with the last equality holding by Lemma
1
, Assumption
10
.3(a), Lemma
5
, and Lemma
7
.

Step 3.

I verify (
8
). Since
m_{0}(z_{i},\gamma_{0})=0
for all
i
by Assumption
5
,
\sup_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}||\Sigma_{0}^{-1}(z,\gamma_{0})||_{op}<\infty
by Assumption
7
, and
\sup_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}||M_{0}(z,\gamma_{0})||_{op}<\infty
by Assumption
6
, it follows from the triangle inequality, Hölder’s inequality, and
||Ax||_{2}\leq||A||_{op}\cdot||x||_{2}
that

\displaystyle\sup_{p_{W|Z}\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}}\left|\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}M_{0}(z_{i},\gamma_{0})^{\prime}\Sigma_{0}^{-1}(z_{i},\gamma_{0})m(z_{i},\gamma_{0})\right|\right|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\sup_{p_{W|Z}\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}}||m(\cdot,\gamma_{0})-m_{0}(\cdot,\gamma_{0})||_{n,2}

\displaystyle=o(n^{-\frac{1}{4}}),

where
C
is the same as Step 2, and
o(n^{-1/4})
by Assumption
10
.3(a).

Step 4.

For (
9
), I first show that
\sup_{p_{W|Z}\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}}||V_{n}-V_{n,0}||_{op}=o(n^{-\frac{1}{4}})
, where
V_{n,0}=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}M_{0}(z_{i},\gamma_{0})^{\prime}\Sigma_{0}^{-1}(z_{i},\gamma_{0})M_{0}(z_{i},\gamma_{0})
. Adding/subtracting
M_{0}(z_{i},\gamma_{0})
and
\Sigma_{0}^{-1}(z_{i},\gamma_{0})
,

\displaystyle V_{n}-V_{n,0}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(M(z_{i},\gamma_{n})-M_{0}(z_{i},\gamma_{0}))^{\prime}\Sigma^{-1}(z_{i},\gamma_{n})M(z_{i},\gamma_{n}^{\dagger})

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}M_{0}(z_{i},\gamma_{0})^{\prime}\Sigma^{-1}(z_{i},\gamma_{n})(M(z_{i},\gamma_{n}^{\dagger})-M_{0}(z_{i},\gamma_{0}))

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}M_{0}(z_{i},\gamma_{0})^{\prime}(\Sigma^{-1}(z_{i},\gamma_{n})-\Sigma_{0}^{-1}(z_{i},\gamma_{0}))M_{0}(z_{i},\gamma_{0}).

The triangle inequality then reveals that it suffices to show three results

\displaystyle\sup_{p_{W|Z}\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left|\left|M_{0}(z_{i},\gamma_{0})^{\prime}(\Sigma(z_{i},\gamma_{n})^{-1}-\Sigma_{0}(z_{i},\gamma_{0})^{-1})M_{0}(z_{i},\gamma_{0})\right|\right|_{op}=o(n^{-\frac{1}{4}})

\displaystyle\sup_{p_{W|Z}\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left|\left|M_{0}(z_{i},\gamma_{0})^{\prime}\Sigma^{-1}(z_{i},\gamma_{n})(M(z_{i},\gamma_{n}^{\dagger})-M_{0}(z_{i},\gamma_{0}))\right|\right|_{op}=o(n^{-\frac{1}{4}})

\displaystyle\sup_{p_{W|Z}\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left|\left|(M(z_{i},\gamma_{n})-M_{0}(z_{i},\gamma_{0}))^{\prime}\Sigma(z_{i},\gamma_{n})^{-1}M(z_{i},\gamma_{n}^{\dagger})\right|\right|_{op}=o(n^{-\frac{1}{4}})

These are verified by applying symmetric arguments to Step 1 of Lemma
6
(see Remark 1 in
Walker (2026a)
). Consequently,
\sup_{p_{W|Z}\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}}\left|\left|V_{n}-V_{n,0}\right|\right|_{op}=o(n^{-\frac{1}{4}})
. It remains to show that
||V_{n,0}-V_{0}||_{op}=o(n^{-1/4})
. Under Assumptions
4
,
6
.3, and
7
.1, the Law of the Iterated Logarithm (applied to each element of
V_{n,0}-V_{0}
) implies that
||V_{n,0}-V_{0}||_{F}=o(n^{-1/4})
, where
||\cdot||_{F}
is Frobenius norm, and
||V_{n,0}-V_{0}||_{op}=o(n^{-1/4})
using
||\cdot||_{op}\leq||\cdot||_{F}
.

Step 5.

Verification of (
10
) follows immediately from the second claim of Lemma
6
because
\gamma_{n},\gamma_{n}^{\dagger}\in B(\gamma_{0},r_{n})
for
n
large and
r_{n}\rightarrow 0
arbitarily slowly as
n\rightarrow\infty
.
∎

C.2.
Proof of Theorem
2

I start with an intermediate lemma (a proof is in
Walker (2026a)
).

Lemma 8
.

Let
S_{n,i}(p_{W|Z})=\tilde{\chi}_{0}(W_{i},z_{i})-E_{P_{W|Z}}[\tilde{\chi}_{0}(W,Z)|Z=z_{i}]
for
i=1,...,n
. If Assumptions
4
,
5
,
6
,
7
,
8
,
9
, and
10
hold, then, for
P_{0,Z}^{\infty}
-almost every fixed realization
\{z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
of
\{Z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
,

\displaystyle\sup_{p_{W|Z}\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}}\left|\log L_{n}(p_{W|Z})-\log L_{n}(p_{t,n,W|Z})-t^{\prime}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}S_{n,i}(p_{W|Z})-\frac{1}{2}t^{\prime}V_{0}^{-1}t\right|\overset{P_{0,W|Z}^{(n)}}{\longrightarrow}0,

where
\{\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}\}_{n\geq 1}
are the sets defined in Assumption
10
and
p_{t,n,W|Z}\propto p_{W|Z}\exp(-t^{\prime}\tilde{\chi}_{0}/\sqrt{n})
.

Proof of Theorem
2
.

The proof has two steps. Step 1 establishes a preliminary convergence result. Step 2 uses this result, Theorem
1
, and Lemma
8
to conclude the BvM.

Step 1.

Let
d_{TV}
be the total variation distance. I show that, for
P_{0,Z}^{\infty}
-almost every fixed realization
\{z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
of
\{Z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
,
d_{TV}(\Pi(p_{W|Z}\in\cdot|W^{(n)}),\Pi(p_{W|Z}\in\cdot|W^{(n)},\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}))\rightarrow 0
in
P_{0,W|Z}^{(n)}
-probability as
n\rightarrow\infty
. The law of total probability and
A\cap\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}^{c}\subseteq\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}^{c}
implies that

\displaystyle\sup_{A}\left|\Pi(p_{W|Z}\in A|W^{(n)})-\Pi(p_{W|Z}\in A\cap\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}|W^{(n)})\right|\leq\Pi\left(p_{W|Z}\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}^{c}\middle|W^{(n)}\right).

Similarly, the definition of conditional probability and complement rule implies

\displaystyle\sup_{A}\left|\Pi(p_{W|Z}\in A\cap\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}|W^{(n)})-\Pi\left(p_{W|Z}\in A\middle|W^{(n)},\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}\right)\right|\leq\Pi\left(p_{W|Z}\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}^{c}\middle|W^{(n)}\right).

Consequently, the triangle inequality and Assumption
10
leads to the conclusion that

\displaystyle d_{TV}\left(\Pi(p_{W|Z}\in\cdot|W^{(n)}),\Pi(p_{W|Z}\in\cdot|W^{(n)},\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z})\right)\leq\Pi\left(p_{W|Z}\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}^{c}\middle|W^{(n)}\right)\overset{P_{0,W|Z}^{(n)}}{\longrightarrow}0

as
n\rightarrow\infty
, for
P_{0,Z}^{\infty}
-almost every fixed realization
\{z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
of
\{Z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
.

Step 2.

Since
d_{BL}\leq d_{TV}
and
\Pi(\sqrt{n}(\gamma_{n}-\hat{\gamma}_{n})\in A|W^{(n)})=\Pi(p_{W|Z}\in\gamma_{n}^{-1}(A_{n})|W^{(n)})
for all events
A
(where
A_{n}=n^{-1/2}A+\hat{\gamma}_{n}
), Step 1 implies that, for
P_{0,Z}^{\infty}
-almost every fixed realization
\{z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
of
\{Z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
,

\displaystyle d_{BL}(\Pi(\sqrt{n}(\gamma_{n}-\hat{\gamma}_{n})\in\cdot|W^{(n)}),\Pi(\sqrt{n}(\gamma_{n}-\hat{\gamma}_{n})\in\cdot|W^{(n)},\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}))\overset{P_{0,W|Z}^{(n)}}{\longrightarrow}0

as
n\rightarrow\infty
. Consequently, it suffices to show, for
P_{0,Z}^{\infty}
-almost every fixed realization
\{z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
of
\{Z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
,

(13)

\displaystyle d_{BL}\left(\Pi\left(\sqrt{n}(\gamma_{n}-\hat{\gamma}_{n})\in\cdot\middle|W^{(n)},\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}\right),\mathcal{N}(0,V_{0}^{-1})\right)\overset{P_{0,W|Z}^{(n)}}{\longrightarrow}0

as
n\rightarrow\infty
, by the triangle inequality. By Lemma 1 of
Castillo and Rousseau (2015)
, (
13
) holds if, for
P_{0,Z}^{\infty}
-almost every fixed realization
\{z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
of
\{Z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
,

\displaystyle\left|E\left[\exp\left(t^{\prime}\sqrt{n}(\gamma_{n}-\hat{\gamma}_{n})\right)\middle|W^{(n)},\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}\right]-\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}t^{\prime}V_{0}^{-1}t\right)\right|\overset{P_{0,W|Z}^{(n)}}{\longrightarrow}0

as
n\rightarrow\infty
for any
t
in a neighborhood of
0
. Adding/subtracting
t^{\prime}\sqrt{n}\gamma_{0}
and
\log L_{n}(p_{t,n,W|Z})
, I know that, for
P_{0,Z}^{\infty}
-almost every fixed realization
\{z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
of
\{Z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
,

\displaystyle t^{\prime}\sqrt{n}(\gamma_{n}-\hat{\gamma}_{n})+\log L_{n}(p_{W|Z})

\displaystyle\quad=t^{\prime}\sqrt{n}(\gamma_{n}-\gamma_{0})-t^{\prime}\sqrt{n}(\hat{\gamma}_{n}-\gamma_{0})+\log L_{n}(p_{W|Z})-\log L_{n}(p_{t,n,W|Z})+\log L_{n}(p_{t,n,W|Z})

\displaystyle\quad=-t^{\prime}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}S_{n,i}(p_{W|Z})+t^{\prime}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}S_{n,i}(p_{W|Z})+\frac{1}{2}t^{\prime}V_{0}^{-1}t+\log L_{n}(p_{t,n,W|Z})+o_{P_{0,W|Z}^{(n)}}(1)

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{1}{2}t^{\prime}V_{0}^{-1}t+\log L_{n}(p_{t,n,W|Z})+o_{P_{0,W|Z}^{(n)}}(1)

uniformly along
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}
, with the second equality using Lemma
8
, Theorem
1
, and the definition of
\hat{\gamma}_{n}
and
p_{t,n,W|Z}
. Hence, for
P_{0,Z}^{\infty}
-almost every fixed realization
\{z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
of
\{Z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
,

\displaystyle E\left[\exp\left(t^{\prime}\sqrt{n}(\gamma_{n}-\hat{\gamma}_{n})\right)\middle|W^{(n)},\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}\right]

\displaystyle\quad=(1+o_{P_{0,W|Z}^{(n)}}(1))\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}t^{\prime}V_{0}^{-1}t\right)\frac{\int_{\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}}\exp\left(\log L_{n}(p_{t,n,W|Z})\right)d\Pi(p_{W|Z})}{\int_{\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n,W|Z}}\exp\left(\log L_{n}(p_{W|Z})\right)d\Pi(p_{W|Z})}

\displaystyle\quad=(1+o_{P_{0,W|Z}^{(n)}}(1))\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}t^{\prime}V_{0}^{-1}t\right)

as
n\rightarrow\infty
, where the last equality applies (
4
). This completes the proof.
∎

C.3.
Proofs of Corollaries

Proof of Corollary
1
.

Theorem 2 and the delta method implies that
\Pi(f(\gamma_{n})\in\cdot|W^{(n)})
satisfies
d_{BL}(\Pi\left(\sqrt{n}(f(\gamma_{n})-f(\hat{\gamma}_{n}))\in\cdot|W^{(n)}),\mathcal{N}\left(0,\Omega_{0,f}\right)\right)\rightarrow 0
in
P_{0,W|Z}^{(n)}
-probability for
P_{0,Z}^{\infty}
-almost every fixed realization
\{z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
of
\{Z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
as
n\rightarrow\infty
,
where
\Omega_{0,f}=\frac{\partial}{\partial\gamma^{\prime}}f(\gamma_{0})V_{0}^{-1}\frac{\partial}{\partial\gamma^{\prime}}f(\gamma_{0})^{\prime}
. Lemma 2 in
Castillo and Rousseau (2015)
then implies that, for
P_{0,Z}^{\infty}
-almost every fixed realization
\{z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
of
\{Z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
,
\Pi(\sqrt{n}(f(\gamma_{n})-f(\hat{\gamma}_{n}))\leq x|W^{(n)})=P_{\mathcal{N}(0,\Omega_{0,f})}(X\leq x)+o_{P_{0,W|Z}^{(n)}}(1)
holds for every
x\in\mathbb{R}
as
n\rightarrow\infty
. Applying Lemma 21.2 of
van der Vaart (1998)
and the equivariance of quantiles to monotone transformations,
\sqrt{n}(c_{n,f}(q)-f(\hat{\gamma}_{n}))=z_{f}(q)+o_{P_{0,W|Z}^{(n)}}(1)
for
P_{0,Z}^{\infty}
-almost every fixed realization
\{z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
of
\{Z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
, where
z_{f}(q)
is the
q
-quantile of
\mathcal{N}\left(0,\Omega_{0,f}\right)
. The claim then follows because
z_{f}(q)=\Phi^{-1}(q)\sqrt{\Omega_{0,f}}
for any
q\in(0,1)
.
∎

Proof of Corollary
2
.

Let
A_{n}(\xi)=\{d_{BL}(\Pi(\sqrt{n}(\gamma_{n}-\hat{\gamma}_{n})\in\cdot|W^{(n)}),\mathcal{N}(0,V_{0}^{-1}))\geq\xi\}
for
\xi>0
. Theorem 2 implies
P_{0,W|Z}^{(n)}(A_{n}(\xi))\rightarrow 0
as
n\rightarrow\infty
for
P_{0,Z}^{\infty}
-almost every fixed realization
\{z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
of
\{Z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
. The Bounded Convergence Theorem then yields that
E_{P_{0,Z}^{\infty}}[P_{0,W|Z}^{(n)}(A_{n}(\xi))]\rightarrow 0
as
n\rightarrow\infty
. Applying the law of iterated expectations and using independence,
E_{P_{0,Z}^{\infty}}[P_{0,W|Z}^{(n)}(A_{n}(\xi))]=E_{P_{0,Z}^{n}}[P_{0,W|Z}^{(n)}(A_{n}(\xi))]=P_{0,WZ}^{n}(A_{n}(\xi))
, so
P_{0,WZ}^{n}(A_{n}(\xi))\rightarrow 0
as
n\rightarrow\infty
. Since
\xi>0
was arbitrary, the proof is complete.
∎

Remark 6
.

An inspection of the proof of Corollary
1
reveals that if Corollary
2
is invoked, then the asymptotic expansion of
c_{n,f}(q)
holds unconditionally too (i.e., replacing
P_{0,W|Z}^{(n)}
with
P_{0,WZ}^{n}
). Hence, the equitailed probability intervals achieve unconditional asymptotic coverage equal to
1-\alpha
too (and have shortest length).

Online Appendix to ‘Semiparametric Bayesian Inference for a Conditional Moment Equality Model’

Additional Discussion Related to Section 3

. Changes to

1.2.
Deadweight Loss Parameter

I derive the DWL parameter. Following equation (1.1) in
Hausman and Newey (1995)
,
S(p^{0},y,\gamma)
solves the differential equation

\displaystyle\frac{\partial S(t,y,\gamma)}{\partial t}=-\exp(\gamma_{0})p(t)^{\gamma_{1}}(y-S(t,y,\gamma))^{\gamma_{2}}\frac{\partial p(t)}{\partial t},\quad S(1,y,\gamma)=0,

where
t\mapsto p(t)
,
t\in[0,1]
, is a continuously differentiable price path with
p(0)=p^{0}
and
p(1)=p^{1}
. Consequently, one can show that

\displaystyle S(p^{0},y,\gamma)=-\left(\frac{(1-\gamma_{2})}{(1+\gamma_{1})y^{\gamma_{2}}}\left(p^{0}q(p^{0},y,\gamma)-p^{1}q(p^{1},y,\gamma)\right)+y^{1-\gamma_{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{1-\gamma_{2}}}+y.

This implies the DWL from a price change from
p^{0}
to
p^{1}
at income
y
is given by

\displaystyle DWL(p^{0},p^{1},y,\gamma)

\displaystyle=y-\left(\frac{(1-\gamma_{2})}{(1+\gamma_{1})y^{\gamma_{2}}}\left(p^{0}q(p^{0},y,\gamma)-p^{1}q(p^{1},y,\gamma)\right)+y^{1-\gamma_{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{1-\gamma_{2}}}

\displaystyle\quad-(p^{1}-p^{0})\exp(\gamma_{0})(p^{1})^{\gamma_{1}}y^{\gamma_{2}}.

Deadweight loss as a fraction of income is
DWL(p^{0},p^{1},y,\gamma)/y
, while deadweight loss as a proportion of tax paid is
DWL(p^{0},p^{1},y,\gamma)/[(p^{1}-p^{0})q(p^{1},y,\gamma)]
.

2.
Restatement of Assumptions

I restate the assumptions for the readers convenience.

Assumption 1
.

For each
n\geq 1
and almost every fixed realization
\{z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
of
\{Z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
, the mapping
(w^{(n)},p_{W|Z})\mapsto\prod_{i=1}^{n}p_{W|Z}(w_{i}|z_{i})
is measurable on
(\mathcal{W}^{n}\times\mathcal{P}_{W|Z},\mathscr{W}^{\otimes n}\otimes\mathscr{P}_{W|Z})
, where
\mathscr{W}
is a
\sigma
-algebra over
\mathcal{W}
, and
\mathscr{W}^{\otimes n}
is the corresponding product
\sigma
-algebra.

Assumption 2
.

Let
p_{0,W|Z}
be the true conditional density. For each
n\geq 1
and almost every fixed realization
\{z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
of
\{Z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
,
P_{0,W|Z}^{(n)}(\int\prod_{i=1}^{n}p_{W|Z}(W_{i}|z_{i})d\Pi(p_{W|Z})\in(0,\infty))=1
, where
P_{0,W|Z}^{(n)}=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}P_{0,W|z_{i}}
(and
P_{0,W|z_{i}}
is the distribution attached to
p_{0,W|Z}(\cdot|z_{i})
).

Assumption 3
.

For almost every fixed realization
\{z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
of
\{Z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
and for each
n\geq 1
, the limit
\gamma_{n}
exists for each
p_{W|Z}\in\mathcal{P}_{W|Z}
and
p_{W|Z}\mapsto\gamma_{n}
is
\mathscr{P}_{W|Z}
-measurable.

Assumption 4
.

1.
\{(W_{i}^{\prime},Z_{i}^{\prime})^{\prime}\}_{i\geq 1}
is an i.i.d sequence with common distribution
P_{0,WZ}=P_{0,W|Z}\otimes P_{0,Z}
, where
P_{0,W|Z}
is the conditional distribution of
W
given
Z
, and
P_{0,Z}
is the marginal distribution of
Z
; 2.
P_{0,W|Z}
has a density
p_{0,W|Z}
.

Assumption 5
.

1.
\Gamma
is a compact subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d_{\gamma}}
,
d_{\gamma}<\infty
; 2. there is a unique
\gamma_{0}\in\text{int}(\Gamma)
such that
m_{0}(z,\gamma_{0})=0
for every
z\in\mathcal{Z}
, where
m_{0}(z,\gamma)
denotes
m(z,\gamma)
at
P_{0,W|Z}
.

Assumption 6
.

1.
\sup_{(z,\gamma)\in\mathcal{Z}\times\Gamma}||m_{0}(z,\gamma)||_{2}<\infty
; 2.
m_{0}(z,\gamma)
is twice continuously differentiable in
\gamma
in a neighborhood
\Gamma_{0}
of
\gamma_{0}
for each
z\in\mathcal{Z}
,
\sup_{(z,\gamma)\in\mathcal{Z}\times\Gamma_{0}}||M_{0}(z,\gamma)||_{op}<\infty
, and
\sup_{(z,\gamma)\in\mathcal{Z}\times\Gamma_{0}}||\partial vec(M_{0}(z,\gamma))/\partial\gamma^{\prime}||_{op}<\infty
, where
M_{0}(z,\gamma)
is the Jacobian of
m_{0}(z,\gamma)
in
\gamma
.

Assumption 7
.

Let
\Sigma_{0}(z,\gamma)
denote
\Sigma(z,\gamma)
at
P_{0,W|Z}
